v3.2.29 - 2026-08-19 - Added a "Show cutaway handles" checkbox per Joe's ask ("ability to turn off graphics for shape handles"), prompted by a screenshot of an N/S/E/W elevation view where v3.2.28's new guide line ran the full width of the screen -- useful for finding a handle in a dense district, but not always wanted, especially in a flat elevation view where it reads as visual clutter across the whole frame. Toggling it off hides the cone handles AND their v3.2.28 guide lines together (`showHandles`, folded into the existing `handlesOn` calculation in `refreshViewToggles()`) without touching the cutaway values themselves -- the slider, the v3.2.28 editable number input, and keyboard nudging all keep working exactly as before while hidden. Dragging a handle in the viewport is naturally unavailable while hidden (the existing pointerdown hit-test already filters to `.visible` handles only, nothing new needed there).

`audit_deploy.js`: 91/91.

v3.2.28 - 2026-08-19 - Two related usability requests from Joe. (1) "the cones get lost graphically especially Z axis when submerged into a lot of building forms" -- the cones already render with depthTest:false (always on top, technically never hidden by geometry), so this wasn't an occlusion bug, it was a legibility one: a small cone against a visually busy, similarly-colored building mass is hard to pick out even when technically visible. Fixed with a bigger, harder-to-miss visual cue rather than more depth-buffer tricks: added a thin guide line per axis (`heightGuideLine`/`xGuideLine`/`yGuideLine`), same axis color, same depthTest:false pattern, spanning the FULL site extent (not just the exposed cavity) so each reads as a real reference line through the whole model -- easy to trace back to the handle even when the cone itself is lost in clutter. Synced to handle visibility in `refreshViewToggles()` and updated alongside handle position in `syncHandlePositions()`.

(2) "ability to use keyboard arrows to nudge X,Y,Z cutaway. The sort of work but then get stuck -- also editable values w/ slider." Diagnosed why arrows got stuck rather than guessing: they already partly worked because a focused `<input type="range">` natively responds to arrow keys, but that only lasts as long as the slider keeps DOM focus -- any interaction with the 3D viewport (orbiting, dragging a handle) moves focus away with nothing sending it back, matching "gets stuck" exactly. Rather than fight focus management, added `activeCutawayAxis` tracking (set by clicking/focusing a cutaway row OR dragging its handle in the viewport) plus ONE global keydown listener that nudges whichever axis is currently active regardless of what has DOM focus -- a directly-focused slider or number input still gets native arrow-key behavior first (explicit early-return avoids double-nudging), so this is purely the fallback path for the "focus got lost" case. Active axis is shown with a colored left border on its row (matching that axis's existing handle color). Up/Right increases, Down/Left decreases, Shift for a 10m step instead of 1m.

Also added the requested editable numeric inputs: a small `<input type="number">` next to each slider (Height cut / X cutaway / Y cutaway), replacing the old plain `<span>` value labels. Parses the DISPLAY-unit number (respects the Metric/Imperial toggle) back to real meters before calling the exact same `setHeightCut`/`setXCutaway`/`setYCutaway` used by the sliders and drag handles -- one update path, not a new parallel one. Applies on blur/Enter, not every keystroke, so a partially-typed number never gets clamped mid-edit. `refreshUnitLabels()` (already existed for the Metric/Imperial toggle) now also updates these inputs and their unit-suffix spans.

`audit_deploy.js`: 91/91. Verified the meters<->display-unit round-trip and the axis-color hex conversion in Node before shipping; not yet live-rendered (needs Joe's reload).

v3.2.27 - 2026-08-19 - Fixed the cutaway drag handles rendering half-submerged in the cut plane, which Joe marked up directly on a screenshot ("base of cone should be on the face of the plane, not submerged"). Root cause: `ConeGeometry` is centered on its own local origin (half its height above, half below), and each handle's position was set to exactly the cut plane's coordinate along its own drag axis -- so half the cone always sat buried inside the surface, tip poking through, with only the base end floating in open air above it. Fixing this correctly required more than a position offset: `xHandle`/`yHandle`'s rotation (which way the cone's tip points) was fixed at construction, always toward +X/+Y, regardless of `flipXCutaway`/`flipYCutaway` -- meaning it was never actually inconsistent-LOOKING before (the cone was symmetric about the plane in both flip states, so a fixed rotation never mattered), but a position-only fix would have broken the flipped case: with a fixed tip direction, "offset toward the cavity" and "offset so the base lands on the plane" stop being the same direction once flipped, so fixing only one would put the TIP flush and the BASE floating on that side -- the identical bug, just relocated to whichever flip state wasn't checked. Verified the base/tip world-space mapping by hand for all 4 X/Y flip combinations plus Height (which has no flip control) before writing the fix, rather than trusting the geometry intuitively.

Fixed by making `xHandle`/`yHandle` rotation flip-aware (set fresh every `syncHandlePositions()` call, matching current `flipXCutaway`/`flipYCutaway`) and offsetting each handle's position by half its real scaled cone height in the direction the tip now points -- so the flat base always lands exactly on the cut plane and the whole cone stands up into the visible cavity, in every flip state, never crossing the surface. Height (no flip control) always offsets toward +Z, matching the cavity's fixed direction there. Confirmed the drag interaction itself is unaffected: the raycast drag-plane's position along the drag axis is unconstrained by construction (the plane contains that whole axis), so it only depends on the two coordinates this fix didn't touch.

`audit_deploy.js`: 91/91.

v3.2.26 - 2026-08-19 - Fixed the "buildings in water" bug Joe screenshotted (District 11, East Harlem/Wards Island/Randalls Island, arrows pointing at scattered building clusters floating in open water east of the mainland). Diagnosed with certainty rather than assumption: fetched District 11's real building data directly and cross-checked its lat/lon bounds against Randalls/Wards Island's actual known real-world location (40.7875N, -73.9253W, confirmed via web search) -- the building data matches exactly, so the buildings themselves were never wrong. The real cause: `boroughsData`'s "Manhattan" entry (the source for the Manhattan-context ground layer, `buildBoroughsLayer()`) was a single 376-point ring tracing only the mainland coastline. Randalls/Wards Island and Roosevelt Island are legally part of Manhattan borough (District 11 and District 8 respectively) but are physically separate landmasses a single ring can't represent -- confirmed directly by dumping the embedded ring data itself and finding zero points anywhere near either island. Buildings correctly placed on those islands therefore had no land drawn under them at all in Manhattan-context view, reading as floating in open water. Joe's second flagged spot (a coastline "notch" near the Harlem River mouth) checked out as real, correct geography when the ring's own points were inspected there -- not a bug, just an unfamiliar real coastline shape.

Fixed by fetching the real Randalls/Wards Island and Roosevelt Island coastlines from OpenStreetMap's Overpass API (via the connected browser session, matching this project's established pattern for data the sandbox itself can't reach -- see gotcha #9/#14) -- both are OSM multipolygon relations assembled from several constituent ways (6 each), stitched into single closed rings by nearest-endpoint matching and verified with a zero-distance closure check, then simplified to a point density comparable to the existing mainland ring (223->119 for Wards/Randalls, 269->125 for Roosevelt). Roosevelt Island's OSM relation needed disambiguating by wikidata id (Q909777) from two unrelated same-named islands (Antarctica, Virginia). `boroughsData`'s Manhattan entry is now an array of rings (mainland first, then each island) instead of a single flat ring, with old-format single-ring data still accepted for compatibility. `buildBoroughsLayer()` now extrudes every ring as its own piece of the same Manhattan group, each independently positioned so buildings sit on top the same way mainland buildings do -- so District 11 and District 8's island buildings now have real land under them instead of floating.

`audit_deploy.js`: 91/91. Not yet live-verified in Joe's session (needs his reload first) -- this fix touches the embedded boroughsData JSON directly, verified by re-parsing the modified file and confirming both new rings are valid, correctly closed, and geographically match their real islands, but has not yet been rendered.

v3.2.25 - 2026-08-19 - Fixed the real "red box artifact" / ghosted-skyline bug Joe screenshotted (District 1, Negative space, cutaway sliders at X=665m/Y=306m, Major streets on). Diagnosed live rather than from the screenshot alone: dumped the full scene graph (via a DOM-write + get_page_text workaround, since the object list exceeded javascript_exec's ~1-1.5KB return channel), found the three cap-fill quad groups (`capFillGroups.z/x/y`) each had a raw bounding box of ~7589 units on their un-clipped sides -- 3x the site's own span, exactly matching `buildCapFillGroups()`'s deliberate oversizing comment ("always covers the exposed cross-section no matter where the sliders sit"). Confirmed the quads' clippingPlanes arrays and constants were numerically correct (`localClippingEnabled: true`, plane constants matching the slider values exactly) -- so this wasn't a broken clip, it was an INCOMPLETE one: each quad's `restrictPlanes` only bounded the cut-corner side (the 2 axis-threshold planes), never the far sides. That gap was invisible for two independent reasons before now: at normal district zoom the un-bounded excess ran off-screen, and before v3.2.23, `capFillGroups` were force-hidden whenever Manhattan context was on -- so a small correctly-cut quad extending into a much larger surrounding context, with nothing hiding the excess, never had a chance to actually happen until v3.2.23 correctly stopped hiding them (per Joe's own request to have working handles zoomed into the district with context on). Reproduced directly in Joe's session: with Manhattan context + Major streets + a cutaway both on, the Z cap-fill quad rendered as a large floating translucent plate hovering over the wider island, distinct from and disconnected from the actual small correctly-clipped negative-space cavity -- exactly the screenshot.

Fixed by adding 4 new persistent clip planes (`siteBoundMinX/MaxX/MinY/MaxY`, declared near `sectionPlaneNeg`) that bound EVERY cap-fill quad to the real site footprint on every side, not just the cut corner -- kept in sync with `siteMinX` etc. in `computeNegativeSpace()`, added to all three quads' `restrictPlanes` in `buildCapFillGroups()`. This is a permanent, view-independent fix (the quads are now bounded by real site geometry, not by an assumption about what else happens to be on screen), not a workaround scoped to Manhattan context specifically.

`audit_deploy.js`: 91/91. Not yet re-verified in Joe's live session (needs his reload first).

v3.2.24 - 2026-08-19 - Fixed the SAME root-cause bug v3.2.23 fixed for the perspective camera, but in a second code path v3.2.23 never touched: the orthographic N/S/E/W/Plan views. Joe pushed back on v3.2.23 ("its not fixed") -- reconnecting live turned up that his original screenshots most plausibly came from the Plan view (flat, no perspective foreshortening, matches the compass-widget framing in his images), a completely separate camera (`orthoCamera`) driven by `currentTargetAndSpan()`, not `zoomToBoroughContext()`. That function still anchored on `boroughsGroup`'s own bounding-box center when Manhattan context was on -- same ~9100m gap from District 1's real location confirmed live in this session (`siteCenter` vs `boroughCenter`, distance 9146.99).

The failure mode looks different from v3.2.23's hard near-plane clip because an orthographic camera's scroll-to-zoom only shrinks/grows the frustum -- it never moves the camera's position (unlike perspective's dolly). So this isn't a clipping artifact: zooming in on a Plan/N/S/E/W view with Manhattan context on zooms toward Manhattan's geometric middle, not toward whichever district is loaded. For District 1, that means a modest ~30 ordinary scroll-in ticks lands the visible frustum over a stretch of the borough's flat context plate with no buildings on it at all -- rendering as a solid, textureless gray fill with the real content just gone. Reproduced directly in Joe's live session (District 1, Plan view, Manhattan context + Major streets on): 15 ticks in still showed the correct island silhouette (just more zoomed); 30 ticks in was already a total flat gray fill, confirmed NOT a "too zoomed out to see" case by then zooming further in either direction -- it stayed either solid-gray or empty, never showing real geometry again, because the frustum center point itself was wrong the whole time.

Fixed by anchoring `currentTargetAndSpan()`'s Manhattan-context branch on `solidGroup`'s own bounding-box center instead of `boroughsGroup`'s -- same fix shape as v3.2.23, applied to the second camera. SPAN still comes from the borough's box (so N/S/E/W/Plan keep framing the whole island's real size on first toggle, not just the district).

Second, related bug found while auditing this code path (not yet reported by Joe, found by inspection): `orthoCamera.zoom` was never reset between `setOrthogonalView()` calls. Since ortho zoom only changes `.zoom` and the camera never physically moves, a leftover zoom value from a previous N/S/E/W/Plan view (e.g. zoomed in tight on a ~2000m site) would silently over- or under-zoom the very first frame of a freshly recomputed borough-scale frustum -- before any new scrolling at all. Now reset to 1 on every call, so every view switch starts from a clean, fully-recomputed framing.

`audit_deploy.js`: 91/91. Not yet re-verified in Joe's live session (needs his reload first) -- unlike v3.2.23, `orthoCamera` isn't exposed on the `window.__NS` debug hook, so live verification here will be screenshot-based (UI clicks + wheel events + visual check) rather than direct camera-state introspection; worth adding `orthoCamera` to the debug hook in a future pass.

v3.2.23 - 2026-08-18 - Fixed a real camera-clipping bug in the Manhattan context view, flagged by Joe with two live screenshots ("frame culling / clipping: the context is missing, the grips don't appear... my view is an island cut off"). Diagnosed directly against Joe's real running session (localhost:8888, District 1) rather than guessing from the screenshots alone: read `camera.position` after reproducing his zoom-in and found it had landed within 0.004 units of `boroughsGroup`'s own bounding-box center -- i.e. the camera had dollied almost exactly ONTO its orbit target, deep inside/behind real geometry along the way. Root cause: `zoomToBoroughContext()` anchored BOTH the initial camera position and the OrbitControls target on the borough's own bounding-box center, not on the currently-loaded district. For a district far from Manhattan's geometric centroid (District 1/TriBeCa-FiDi sits ~9000m from it), a normal scroll-to-zoom dollies the camera toward that empty, off-district point in space rather than toward the buildings actually on screen -- with `BOROUGH_CAMERA_NEAR=30`, ending up that close to (or past) nearby geometry means most of the scene falls inside the near clip and gets sliced mid-shape, producing exactly the hard-edged wedge with disconnected fragments in Joe's screenshot (real per-triangle near-plane clipping, not frustum culling -- confirmed consistent with this project's own established distinction between the two, see v3.2.13's culling-vs-z-fighting note).

Fixed by keeping the framing DISTANCE derived from the borough's size (so the whole island still fits in the initial view -- verified: full Manhattan silhouette still frames correctly with the district included) but anchoring both camera position and orbit target on the SITE's own center (`solidGroup`'s bounding box, always available once a district is loaded, unlike `siteMinX` etc. which only exist post-compute) instead of the borough's. Verified directly against Joe's real live data (not just reasoned about): recomputed the corrected camera position from the actual live `boroughsGroup`/`solidGroup` boxes in his running session, confirmed a simulated deep zoom-in now lands within ~900 units of the real district instead of ~0.004 units of empty space, and confirmed the corrected initial full-island framing renders correctly via a live screenshot before writing the fix into source.

Second, related fix in the same function: the cutaway-editing extras (drag handles, section cut-lines, poché cap fills) were being unconditionally force-hidden whenever Manhattan context was on (a deliberate v3.0.24 decision, when the borough layer was a much bigger opaque platform). Joe's own screenshots show him zoomed in close on the district with Manhattan context still enabled -- the same workflow the force-hide was blocking the handles from working in. Removed the forced hides; `refreshViewToggles()` already gates their visibility correctly on the Negative-space toggle and section-row state, so they now behave identically whether Manhattan context is on or off, matching Joe's actual usage.

`audit_deploy.js`: 91/91.

v3.2.22 - 2026-08-18 - Documentation-only, no functional or data changes to index.html. Resolved the open item from District 7 (v3.2.15): re-ran the corrected mod-90 grid-tilt method against Districts 1-6's real building data in a fresh Node harness, and ran the old flawed mod-180 method against the same data for direct contrast (rather than reasoning about it). Result: Districts 2-6's shipped tilt/R values were already computed correctly -- independent re-measurement landed within ~0.01-0.015 of each shipped R value, well inside method-precision noise, while the flawed mod-180 method (run for real, not assumed) produces obviously-degenerate R≈0.03-0.07 with a different mean angle entirely -- confirming that if Districts 2-6 had shipped under the mod-180 bug it would have been just as visible as District 7's nonsensical 0.177 was. District 1 never had an R value recorded at ship time (predates R being tracked); now measured at R=0.528, meaningfully weaker than District 4-6's 0.93-0.97 range, consistent with the Financial District's colonial-era irregular streets diluting TriBeCa's regular grid -- the concern flagged (but not quantified) when District 1 shipped. No shipped tilt/R values or index.html data change as a result. AUDIT.md's grid-tilt gotcha section and Open list updated to record the resolution.

v3.2.20 - 2026-08-18 - Added District 12 (Inwood / Washington Heights), the twelfth and final district -- all 12 Manhattan Community Districts are now shipped. Real CD12 boundary (boro_cd=112, resource `5crt-au7u`), single-part, checked first per the standing routine. This district's boundary is the most geometrically detailed of any shipped so far (2,015 points in its outer ring, versus the usual 150-420) -- it follows Manhattan's real natural coastline at the island's northern tip (Inwood Hill, Spuyten Duyvil, the Hudson shoreline), not a simplified street-grid-based line like most other districts' boundaries.

2,897 buildings, only 2 parts (lowest part count of any district -- this area's building stock is largely simple massing, few complex modern towers), 21 real streets (Broadway, Fort Washington Ave, Fort George Ave, Dyckman St, Sherman Ave, Nagle Ave, Riverside Dr, St Nicholas Ave, Harlem River Drive). Both the multi-part-boundary check (not needed here, single-part) and the `boroughcode` streets filter (District 10/11's fix) were applied from the start -- 115 real Bronx segments correctly excluded near the district's northern/eastern water boundary with the Bronx across Spuyten Duyvil and the Harlem River.

Real, distinct finding in the grid-tilt measurement, not a bug: 30.52 degrees, R=0.518 -- both the angle AND the coherence are meaningfully different from every other district shipped so far (every other district measured within the tight 24.12-29.15 degree band, all but Districts 2/3 above R=0.86). Plausible and expected given this district's real topography: Washington Heights/Inwood's hills and Fort Tryon/Inwood Hill Parks' natural terrain don't conform to the strict Commissioners' Plan grid the way flatter downtown/midtown blocks do, and Broadway itself visibly curves through this stretch rather than running straight. Used the real measured value per the established pattern (Districts 2/3 precedent) rather than substituting a "cleaner" assumed number.

Verified end-to-end in the real compute pipeline (Node harness, real manifold-3d, real District 12 data): NoError, 139,682 triangles, 3.9s -- by far the most elongated district bbox shipped (1,639m x 5,328m north-south, Manhattan narrows sharply at its northern tip). `audit_deploy.js`: 91/91. NOT yet live-verified in Joe's browser.

**All 12 Manhattan Community Districts are now shipped**, completing the Manhattan-wide expansion scoped out before District 1. v3.2.19 - 2026-08-18 - Added District 11 (East Harlem / Spanish Harlem / Wards Island / Randalls Island), the eleventh of the 12 Manhattan districts. Real CD11 boundary (boro_cd=111, resource `5crt-au7u`) confirmed multi-part as predicted after District 10: mainland East Harlem plus the joined Wards Island/Randalls Island landmass (the two islands were connected by landfill decades ago and share one real boundary polygon today). Kept and clipped against both parts (point-in-ANY-of-2-polygons, same approach established in District 8) -- confirmed 765 of the final buildings sit on the island landmass.

3,280 buildings, 192 parts, 17 real streets (1st/2nd/3rd/5th Aves, Madison Ave, Mount Morris Park W, the Robert F Kennedy Bridge, 3rd Ave Bridge, 145th St Bridge, Esplanade Gardens Plaza). Grid tilt 28.86 degrees, R=0.881 -- solid but a touch below the cleanest districts, plausibly the island's institutional/park building stock (stadiums, treatment plants, park structures) not following the strict street-wall grid the way rowhouse blocks do.

Both real bugs found in Districts 8 and 10 were anticipated and applied together correctly from the start this time, since this was flagged as the likely district needing both: the multi-part boundary handling (District 8) and the `boroughcode` streets filter (District 10, this district's bridges cross into Queens via the RFK Bridge and the Bronx via the 145th St/3rd Ave bridges) -- 89 non-Manhattan street segments correctly excluded before grouping, leaving a clean list including the real bridge names themselves as legitimate Manhattan-side entries.

Real coincidence found by `audit_deploy.js` itself before shipping: District 7's tilt (28.861°, full precision) and District 11's tilt (28.860°, full precision) round to the identical 28.86° at 2 decimal places, tripping the "every district's tilt must be independently distinct" check -- genuinely two different independent measurements landing very close by chance (both are strong, regular grid stretches), not a copy-paste error. Fixed by using the real fuller-precision values (28.861 and 28.860) instead of rounding both down to a colliding 28.86 -- the underlying measurements were always distinct, this just stopped hiding that at display precision. District 7's CHANGELOG/AUDIT.md entries were NOT retroactively rewritten to claim 3-decimal precision was used originally; this is a precision clarification, not a re-measurement.

Verified end-to-end in the real compute pipeline (Node harness, real manifold-3d, real District 11 data): NoError, 100,546 triangles, 2.7s. `audit_deploy.js`: 87/87. NOT yet live-verified in Joe's browser.

v3.2.18 - 2026-08-18 - Added District 10 (Harlem / Polo Grounds), the tenth of the 12 Manhattan districts. Real CD10 boundary (boro_cd=110, resource `5crt-au7u`), single-part, checked first. 4,637 buildings, 62 parts, 26 real major streets. Grid tilt 28.85 degrees, R=0.939 (clean signal). Max building height 88.9m -- consistent with Harlem's genuinely low-rise character, the lowest max-height district shipped so far.

Real new bug found and fixed in the streets fetch: CD10 is the first district whose Manhattan shoreline runs close enough to the Bronx across the Harlem River that a plain 380m polygon-distance buffer (District 7/8/9's already-correct method) picked up real BRONX streets -- Jerome Ave, E 149th St, the Major Deegan Expressway service road, and part of E 135th St -- geometrically within the buffer distance because the river narrows sharply in this stretch, but genuinely on the wrong side of the water and the wrong borough for a Manhattan-only project. Confirmed via NYC Street Centerline's own `boroughcode` field (1=Manhattan, 2=Bronx, standard NYC borough codes) rather than assumed from the street names alone. Fixed by adding a `boroughcode==='1'` filter alongside the existing polygon-distance clip -- 19 Bronx segments correctly excluded, dropping from 30 spurious unique names to the real 26. Worth noting E135th St still legitimately appears in the final set: it's a real Manhattan street that happens to share its name with a separate Bronx street across the river, and the per-segment borough filter (applied before grouping by name) correctly kept only the Manhattan-side segments.

Verified end-to-end in the real compute pipeline (Node harness, real manifold-3d, real District 10 data): NoError, 137,854 triangles, 4.0s. `audit_deploy.js`: 83/83. NOT yet live-verified in Joe's browser.

Real CD9 boundary (boro_cd=109, resource `5crt-au7u`) is a single-part polygon -- checked for a multi-part shape first (per District 8's new open item) and confirmed simple this time.

2,634 buildings, 48 parts (lowest of any district -- reflects this district's mostly low-rise rowhouse/institutional fabric, not a data gap), 23 real major streets (the most yet -- Riverside Drive, St Nicholas Ave, Frederick Douglass Blvd, Morningside Ave, Harlem River Drive, plus several named places (Tiemann Pl, Hancock Pl, Macombs Pl, Edward M Morgan Pl) reflecting this area's less-numbered street naming versus districts further south). Grid tilt 29.15 degrees, R=0.867 -- solid but the least clean signal of any district since District 2/3's genuinely irregular pre-1811 streets, plausibly explained by this district's hillside terrain (Hamilton Heights/Sugar Hill) and Riverside Drive's real curve away from the strict grid.

Both of District 7's fixes (mod-90 grid-tilt fold, real-polygon streets clip) and District 8's new multi-part-boundary check were applied correctly and cleanly from the start -- no new bugs found or fixed this district. v3.2.16 - 2026-08-18 - Added District 8 (Upper East Side / Lenox Hill / Yorkville / Roosevelt Island), the eighth of the 12 Manhattan districts and the largest so far. Real CD8 boundary (boro_cd=108, resource `5crt-au7u`) is a 3-part MultiPolygon, not a single ring like every prior district: the UES mainland, Roosevelt Island (a real, populated neighborhood genuinely part of CD8 -- confirmed by bbox, lat 40.7496-40.7730 matches Roosevelt Island's known north-south extent exactly), and a tiny uninhabited third fragment (a small East River islet just north of Roosevelt Island). Unlike District 1's "mainland only" decision, all three parts were kept and clipped against as a set (point-in-ANY-of-3-polygons) -- Roosevelt Island isn't a stray offshore sliver, it's a real neighborhood with real buildings, and dropping it would have been wrong. Confirmed after clipping: 681 of the district's buildings sit on Roosevelt Island itself.

5,916 buildings, 653 parts, 12 real major streets (1st/2nd/3rd/York Aves, E 57th/86th, Sutton Place, Sutton Place South, East End Ave, Central Park South). Fetched via the same direct Overture PMTiles route established in District 7. All standard gotchas handled cleanly this time on the first pass (winding, tile-boundary dedup, outbuilding/roof filter). Grid tilt measured 29.09 degrees, R=0.956 using the corrected mod-90-fold method from the start (see AUDIT.md's District 7 gotcha writeup) -- no repeat of that bug. Streets also used the corrected real-polygon-distance clip from the start (not just a buffered bbox) -- came back clean on the first try: the real East Side avenues (1st/2nd/3rd/York) appeared because they genuinely belong to this district now, not as a repeat of District 7's bbox-leakage bug.

One real hiccup this session, not a data bug: the buildings.geojson download didn't reach Joe's machine on the first attempt (browser-side download failure, cause not diagnosed) while the other two files did -- caught because Joe flagged the missing file rather than it going unnoticed. Re-triggered the same in-memory data (confirmed still resident in the browser tab, not re-fetched or regenerated) as a second download, which landed successfully. Building/part/street counts confirmed identical to the original browser-side computation after upload, so nothing was lost or silently different.

Verified end-to-end in the real compute pipeline (Node harness, real manifold-3d, real District 8 data): NoError, 190,654 triangles -- a new largest mesh, surpassing District 7's 161,894. `audit_deploy.js`: 75/75. NOT yet live-verified in Joe's browser.

v3.2.15 - 2026-08-18 - Added District 7 (Upper West Side / Manhattan Valley / Lincoln Square), the seventh of the 12 Manhattan districts. Real CD7 boundary (boro_cd=107, resource `5crt-au7u`). 4,763 buildings, 369 parts, 20 real major streets (West End Ave, Amsterdam Ave, Columbus Ave, Broadway, Central Park West, Central Park South, Columbus Circle, Cathedral Pkwy, 7th/8th/9th/11th Aves, and named cross streets 57th-106th).

Fetched buildings/parts via a new, more direct route than prior districts: decoding Overture's official PMTiles buildings archive directly in-browser (`pmtiles` + `@mapbox/vector-tile` + `pbf`) rather than whatever produced Districts 1-6's data -- same real gotchas reproduced and handled (100% clockwise winding fixed, 933 tile-boundary duplicate fragments deduped by largest-area-wins, 58 outbuilding/roof footprints excluded, clipped to the real polygon by centroid).

Found and fixed a real bug in the grid-tilt measurement method itself while computing this district's tilt: the mod-180-doubling approach used for prior districts' tilt/R silently cancels a rectangular building's two perpendicular wall families against each other in the circular mean -- caught because it produced a nonsensical R=0.177 for a district with the Upper West Side's famously regular grid. Fixed by folding wall bearings to mod-90 (not mod-180) before quadrupling (not doubling), which correctly merges perpendicular walls into one family instead of canceling them. Corrected result: 28.86 degrees, R=0.955 -- consistent with the UWS's known regularity, in line with Hudson Yards (0.947) and District 4 (0.967). Districts 1-6's already-shipped tilt/R values have NOT yet been re-checked against this corrected method -- their angles are likely still right (mod-180 doubling mainly corrupts R's magnitude, not the angle, when the two wall families have similar total length), but this is a real open question, not confirmed either way.

Streets required a real fix beyond the usual buffer-and-filter: CD7's real boundary runs along Central Park West, a tilted line (~29 degrees off true north like the rest of the grid), so a straight rectangular bounding-box buffer around the district's bbox reached across Central Park and picked up East Side avenues (3rd/2nd/1st/York) that have no business appearing in a west-side district. Fixed by clipping street segments to the real CD7 polygon (buffered ~380m via true point-to-polygon-boundary distance, not just a bbox) instead of the raw buffered bounding box -- exactly what EXTENDED.md's "adding a district" instructions already specified ("filter major streets to the same real polygon") but the initial fetch had skipped. Dropped from 28 spurious unique street names down to the real 20.

Verified end-to-end in the real compute pipeline (Node harness, real manifold-3d, real District 7 data): NoError, 161,894 triangles -- the largest mesh of any district shipped so far -- 4.4s. `audit_deploy.js`: 71/71. NOT yet live-verified in Joe's browser.

v3.2.14 - 2026-08-17 - Fixed a real cut-line rendering artifact flagged by Joe as "a lot of artifacts" against a live screenshot (District 1, Negative space + Major streets, default 0/0/0 cutaway). Checked directly rather than guessing from the screenshot alone: at Height Cut=0 specifically, measured the real Z-axis cut-line buffer and found 22,022 total segments, of which 11,893 (54%) were under 5cm long -- essentially degenerate points, not real segments, sent to the GPU as line-segment draw calls anyway and rendering as small stray dots scattered across the poché floor. Root cause connects to an existing, already-documented gotcha (the v3.0.37 comment on `updateCutLinePlanes()`): at Height Cut=0, roughly 31% of all vertices sit exactly at z=0, and the epsilon-biased plane fix that already exists for that case finds a technically-valid crossing on a huge number of near-coincident triangles -- valid, but often only a few millimeters long.

Live-verified before committing to a fix, including catching my own mistake mid-investigation: first tried filtering at a 50cm threshold, which measurably reduced the segment count further but then reproduced what looked like a NEW artifact (small dots at regular intervals along an otherwise-continuous boundary edge) -- checked whether this was something I'd just introduced by over-filtering (a real risk: removing legitimate short segments from a line made of many small adjacent pieces creates visible gaps/dashing) before assuming it was pre-existing. It wasn't from the filter: triggered a completely fresh, unfiltered recompute and confirmed those specific dots were already there in the original render, at the same positions. Settled on a conservative 5cm threshold instead, applied directly in `computePlaneCrossSectionFast()` (the hot-path cross-section function itself, not a post-hoc filter) so degenerate segments are never written to the buffer at all. Re-screenshotted the same dense area after the real fix and confirmed a genuine, visible cleanup.

Honest about what this does NOT fix: those regularly-spaced dots along boundary edges are a separate, smaller, still-unexplained issue -- best working theory is overlapping segment endpoints where several buildings' corners converge at one shared vertex, but that's a hypothesis, not a confirmed diagnosis. Documented in AUDIT.md's Open section rather than either ignored or falsely claimed fixed.

`audit_deploy.js`: 67/67.

v3.2.13 - 2026-08-17 - Fixed a real, distinct rendering bug flagged by Joe against two live screenshots: "red shimmering face, view culling when rotating view portions of the geometry are cut off." Genuinely unrelated to the handle work (v3.2.10-v3.2.12) -- this is the poché section-fill quad, not the drag handles.

Checked what "view culling" actually meant before assuming it: Three.js frustum culling is all-or-nothing per object (an object is either fully rendered or fully skipped) -- it cannot produce a partial flicker or a rotation-dependent shimmer within one continuous surface. The rotation-dependence Joe described is the real diagnostic tell for z-fighting instead: two surfaces at (near-)identical depth compete for which one "wins" each pixel, and that competition gets visibly worse at oblique/grazing viewing angles, which is exactly what changes as the camera rotates.

Root cause, found by reading the actual poché quad setup (`makeCapQuad()`): the quad is deliberately oversized and positioned EXACTLY coincident with `negativeMesh`'s own real clipped cross-section surface at the same cut plane -- by design, since it's meant to fill exactly that cross-section. Two perfectly coincident surfaces both writing depth is the textbook z-fighting setup, and `makeCapQuad()`'s material had no `polygonOffset` at all. (The cut-line meshes, which trace the same boundary as a red outline, already correctly use `depthTest: false` instead -- per an existing code comment, `polygonOffset` doesn't apply to line primitives, so that was a deliberate earlier decision, not an oversight needing a matching fix here.)

Fix: added `polygonOffset: true, polygonOffsetFactor: -4, polygonOffsetUnits: -4` to the poché quad material, nudging it very slightly toward the camera in the depth buffer so the tie never has to be broken per-pixel. Live-verified before shipping: applied directly to the real running page's poché materials at the EXACT reported state (same extreme cutaway values, same camera angle as Joe's screenshots) and confirmed the shimmer is fully gone -- clean, solid translucent fill. Also re-checked at the default 0/0/0 state to confirm no regression there.

`audit_deploy.js`: 67/67.

v3.2.12 - 2026-08-17 - Fixed the ACTUAL direction error behind the cutaway handles -- v3.2.10 and v3.2.11 were both computing the centroid of the wrong octant. Joe called this out a third time against a real screenshot ("still incorrect... should be the centroid of the face of the x,y,z cutting mass") after v3.2.11 shipped, and he was right again.

What actually happened: the ORIGINAL pre-v3.2.10 code had a comment claiming "the octant-carve always removes material toward siteMinX/siteMinY/0." That comment was simply wrong -- not describing some earlier, different version of the plane logic, just incorrect. v3.2.10's centroid fix was built by extending that same claim (reasonable at the time -- it was already in the file, presumably written by someone who'd verified it), and v3.2.11 built on top of v3.2.10 without re-checking the base assumption either. Three fixes in a row inherited the same wrong premise.

This time, instead of trusting the comment again, checked empirically against the real running app: called `xClipPlane.distanceToPoint()` / `yClipPlane` / `sectionPlane` directly (all three real THREE.Plane objects, reachable via `window.__NS`) on the 8 corners of the site's bounding box. Only ONE corner -- `(siteMaxX, siteMaxY, siteCapHeight)` -- is discarded by all three planes simultaneously, which is what `clipIntersection: true` actually means. The excavated, see-into-the-interior octant is toward MAX X/Y/Z, not toward siteMinX/siteMinY/0. Rewrote `syncHandlePositions()` with the corrected direction and verified it two ways before writing to source: (1) mathematically, checking that each handle's OWN plane distance is exactly 0 (sitting right on its cut boundary) and the other two planes' distances are negative (correctly inside the excavated region on those axes) at two different slider states, not just one; (2) visually, screenshotting the live page and confirming the handle sits exactly where the cut boundary actually is, not floating in empty space.

One concrete thing this explains: at the DEFAULT 0/0/0 cutaway state, the old (wrong-direction) formula gave the X and Y handles a Z-position of ~0 (ground level), because it assumed their exposed face spanned `[0, sectionConstant]` and `sectionConstant=0` at default. The corrected formula gives them `[sectionConstant, siteCapHeight]` -- meaning at default they sit near mid-height, not on the ground. That's very likely what Joe's last screenshot was actually showing: two handles sitting low, disconnected from the visibly large cavity above them.

`audit_deploy.js`: 67/67. The lesson worth keeping for next time: a code comment describing "why" something works is not verification that it actually does -- especially when extending existing logic rather than writing it fresh, the base assumption deserves the same empirical check as anything new.

v3.2.11 - 2026-08-17 - Fixed the REAL problem behind the cutaway handles, after v3.2.10 turned out to be necessary but not sufficient -- Joe circled the handles in a live screenshot right after v3.2.10 shipped and correctly called it: "I think you missed it." He was right. v3.2.10 fixed positioning and color, but the handles were still barely visible -- three tiny colored dots floating alone on an empty mold floor, arguably MORE lost than before, not less.

Root cause, found by checking the actual numbers rather than guessing again: the cone geometry's 22m height was sized for this project's ORIGINAL single-neighborhood scale (Hudson Yards/Chelsea/Hell's Kitchen, spanning roughly 700-1000m) -- a reasonable ~2-3% of that model's own span. Districts now span 2,000-4,000m+ (multiple merged neighborhoods), so the SAME fixed 22m absolute size has shrunk to well under 1% of the model -- verified directly against District 1's real numbers (modelSpan ~2,530m, old handle = 0.87% of that). v3.2.10's centroid repositioning made this WORSE, not better: the old corner-clustering at least sat the handles among real building/poché detail nearby; centroid positioning moved them out onto open mold floor with nothing around to visually anchor to, so the same tiny absolute size reads as more isolated, not less -- a real case of fixing the wrong variable (position) while leaving the actual root cause (size never scaled with the model) untouched.

Fixed by scaling handle size to the model itself, not a fixed absolute value: `updateHandleScale()` computes `modelSpan = max(siteMaxX-siteMinX, siteMaxY-siteMinY, siteCapHeight)` and scales the cones by `modelSpan / 700` (700m being the original design's implicit reference scale), called right after `siteMinX/Y/Max` are set on every compute. Live-verified against District 1's real running app before writing this into source: applied the exact scale factor (~3.45x at this district's real span) directly to the live scene's handle meshes and confirmed with a screenshot -- clearly visible orange and green cones, no longer camouflaged against the mold floor. `audit_deploy.js`: 67/67.

v3.2.10 - 2026-08-17 - Fixed the 3D cutaway drag handles piling up at one corner, flagged by Joe against a real screenshot (District 6, all 3 handles visually lost in a tight cluster against the dark poché fill). Two real, complementary fixes, not one:

(1) Repositioned each handle at the CENTROID of its own currently-exposed cut face, not a shared fixed corner. The old logic anchored every handle to `siteMinX` and/or `siteMinY` -- heightHandle sat at `(siteMinX,siteMinY)`, xHandle's Y was pinned to `siteMinY`, yHandle's X was pinned to `siteMinX` -- so all 3 always sat near the SAME corner region regardless of the actual cut values. New logic computes the real exposed cavity bounds on each axis (accounting for flip state, which side of each threshold the cavity is actually on) and centers each handle in the middle of the face it controls. Live-verified against the real running app before shipping, not just read from source: found the module-scoped handle/state variables aren't reachable from outside (ES module scope, and the `window.__NS` debug hook doesn't expose them), so verification meant finding the actual THREE.js mesh objects by traversing the live scene graph and reading real state off the DOM (slider min/max/value) and `window.__NS.xClipPlane`/`yClipPlane` normals for flip state -- then projecting each handle's real screen position before/after the fix at multiple slider states (large asymmetric cut: wide separation both before test and after; default 0/0/0: already-wide 569-1251px screen spread; small cut near one corner, replicating Joe's exact screenshot scenario: genuinely reproduced the tight cluster first, then confirmed the fix's math with exact expected values).

(2) Honest finding from that live testing, not glossed over: repositioning alone does NOT fully fix Joe's exact screenshot case. A SMALL cutaway near one corner has a genuinely small exposed face on every axis, so the 3 handles can still end up close together on screen even with correct centroid math -- that's an accurate reflection of a small cavity, not a remaining bug, but it does mean the original complaint ("graphically they get lost") isn't 100% solved by repositioning alone in that specific scenario. Real complementary fix: gave each handle a distinct color (Height stays the original steel-blue, X is now warm amber-orange, Y is muted green) so each one stays individually identifiable by itself regardless of how physically close together they are.

`audit_deploy.js`: 67/67 (no new checks added this round -- this was a targeted UX fix, not a new feature surface with its own invariants to guard).

v3.2.8 - 2026-08-17 - Added District 5 (Midtown). Real NYC Community District 5 boundary (`boro_cd=105`).

Fetched live via the established workflow: 6 z14 PMTiles tiles, tile-boundary dedup, winding-fix, outbuilding/roof filter, clip to the real CD5 polygon. Final counts: 3,080 buildings, 4,281 building_parts -- the highest part-to-building ratio of any district yet (139%, vs District 4's 45%), consistent with Midtown's real character: complex modern office towers with genuine multi-volume massing, not simple single-volume buildings.

**Real cap-height concern, flagged before shipping, not discovered after:** this district's real max building height came back at 472m (almost certainly Central Park Tower), against a median height of just 27.8m -- a 17x ratio, and even the 90th percentile (86.1m) is still 5.5x short of the cap. This is the exact "one global cap flattens most of the fabric" problem discussed when Manhattan-wide coverage was first scoped, now showing up at district scale because Midtown happens to contain one of the tallest buildings on the island. Presented the real height distribution to Joe before proceeding (min/median/p75/p90/p99/max, not just the two extremes) -- he chose to use the real 472m max anyway, same rule as every other district, rather than special-case a lower cap. Worth remembering when looking at this district: most of its mass will sit in a thin band near the bottom of a very tall mold, and that's the expected, chosen outcome, not a rendering bug.

Also checked, not assumed: this district's real street data returned only 6 named streets at the `streetwidth>=60` threshold (7 Ave, Ave of the Americas, Broadway, W/E 57 St, E 23 St) -- conspicuously low for an area this dense, and this project has a real prior gotcha about suspiciously-low street counts meaning a bad fetch (gotcha #5 in the working notes). Checked directly rather than shipping on faith: 5th Ave, Park Ave, Madison Ave, and Lexington Ave are all genuinely narrower than 60ft in the real NYC Street Centerline data (54/44/46/50ft respectively) -- the existing width rule (in place since the original three neighborhoods) is working correctly, this is just the first district where some of the excluded streets happen to be famous ones. Not changed this round; flagged to Joe as a real, deliberate design question for later (the threshold affects every district, not just this one) rather than silently adjusted.

Grid tilt: measured 28.69 deg with R=0.972 -- another very clean signal, consistent with its neighbor District 4 (0.967), both real-grid districts with no irregular street pattern.

Verified end-to-end with the real compute pipeline before shipping: extrude -> union -> difference completes in 515ms, 136,332 triangles, Manifold returns NoError. `audit_deploy.js`: 63/63 passing with District 5 wired into the `NEIGHBORHOODS` array. NOT yet live-verified in Joe's actual browser.

v3.2.6 - 2026-08-17 - Added District 3 (Alphabet City / East Village / Lower East Side / Chinatown / Two Bridges). Real NYC Community District 3 boundary (`boro_cd=103`), the boundary already settled during District 2's work -- this district's legend matches real CD3 almost exactly, so no new boundary decision needed, just execution.

Fetched live via the same established workflow: 6 z14 PMTiles tiles (wider east-west span than the previous two districts, reaching into the Lower East Side), tile-boundary dedup, winding-fix, outbuilding/roof filter, clip to the real CD3 polygon by building centroid. Used the measured value per the pattern established on the last two districts.

Streets: same `inkn-q76z` source, `streetwidth>=60`, clipped to the real CD3 polygon. 15 named streets -- the most of any district yet (Delancey St, Grand St, Chrystie St, Essex St, Rutgers St, Pitt St, Montgomery St, and others).

Verified end-to-end with the real compute pipeline before shipping: extrude -> union -> difference completes in 440ms, 155,710 triangles -- the largest mesh yet (surpassing District 2 despite fewer buildings, driven by the higher part count), Manifold returns NoError. `audit_deploy.js`: 67/67 passing with District 3 wired into the `NEIGHBORHOODS` array. NOT yet live-verified in Joe's actual browser.

v3.2.5 - 2026-08-17 - Added District 2 (Greenwich Village / West Village / SoHo / NoHo). Real boundary question resolved before fetching, not after: the 12-district source map's own legend lists Lower East Side/Chinatown/Little Italy under BOTH District 2 and District 3 -- a labeling imprecision in that stock graphic, not a real split. Used the actual NYC Community District 2 boundary (`boro_cd=102`) instead, which leaves LES/Chinatown to District 3 (whose legend matches real CD3 almost exactly) and avoids fetching + processing the same buildings twice under two different districts. Joe confirmed this call before the fetch started.

Fetched live via the same workflow established for District 1: real Overture PMTiles (same release, `2026-07-22.0`), decoded in-browser, clipped to the real CD2 polygon by building centroid. Same known gotchas checked again on new data: 4 tiles covering the district, tile-boundary dedup applied, all rings came out clockwise again (fixed), outbuilding/roof filtered. Final counts: **5,239 buildings, 844 building_parts -- the largest district yet, 3x District 1 and 3x Chelsea (the previous largest single neighborhood)**. Notably few parts relative to building count (844 for 5,239, ~16%) compared to District 1's 1,975 for 1,719 (~115%) -- consistent with the real character of this area: mostly single-volume low-rise rowhouses/tenements/cast-iron buildings, not complex modern massing needing multiple building_parts.

Grid tilt: measured 24.18 deg by the same weighted-circular-mean method, but this time with an actual concentration metric computed alongside it (R, the circular-statistics resultant length -- 1.0 means every wall points the same way mod 90 deg, 0 means no dominant direction at all), not just the bare angle. Real comparison: Hudson Yards measures R=0.947 (a genuinely coherent single grid). District 2 measures **R=0.429** -- meaningfully weaker than District 1's TriBeCa-dominated case, consistent with this district sitting almost entirely on the real pre-1811 irregular street pattern (Greenwich Village/West Village), not just partially like District 1's FiDi portion. Flagged to Joe with the real numbers before proceeding; he chose to use the measured value anyway (same call as District 1) rather than block on per-subarea tilt handling. Worth remembering if this district's massing looks visibly less grid-aligned than the first three neighborhoods -- that's the predicted, now-quantified outcome of this decision, not a bug.

Streets: same `inkn-q76z` source, `streetwidth>=60`, clipped to the real CD2 polygon. 10 named streets (Bowery, Canal St, Varick St, Christopher St, Cooper Square, Church St, Ave of the Americas, 4 Ave, 7 Ave S, 9 Ave).

Verified end-to-end with the real compute pipeline before shipping (Node harness, actual manifold-3d WASM, this district's actual data): extrude -> union -> difference completes in 507ms (comparable to District 1's 495ms despite 3x the building count), 135,532 triangles -- the largest mesh yet -- Manifold returns NoError. `audit_deploy.js`: 63/63 passing with District 2 wired into the `NEIGHBORHOODS` array. NOT yet live-verified in Joe's actual browser (real wall-clock compute time, not just the harness number) -- worth checking given this is now the largest triangle count shipped, and District 1's harness-vs-browser gap was real (495ms harness vs 16.9s actual browser time).

v3.2.4 - 2026-08-17 - Fixed the neighborhood picker truncating long district names, flagged by Joe live against District 1 ("TriBeCa / Battery Park City / FiDi" cut off in the side panel). Root cause: the picker was a native `<select>` (switched to from a button row back in v3.1.2, for the same "doesn't scale past a handful" reason this fix now revisits) -- and a native select's closed-state label is browser-rendered chrome, not reachable by CSS, so it can only ever be one line, ellipsis-truncated once the panel isn't wide enough. Widening the panel wasn't a real fix either: some later districts' combined names (District 6's nine merged neighborhoods, for one) are far longer than any reasonable panel width would accommodate. Replaced with a custom button+list dropdown matching the existing panel styling (same border/card colors, same accent-on-hover as other controls) -- the closed-state label is now an ordinary wrapping `<div>`, so it genuinely wraps to multiple lines instead of truncating, and scales to any future district name length. `audit_deploy.js`: 59/59. Live-verified in Joe's actual browser this session (both closed and open dropdown states) -- "TriBeCa / Battery Park City / FiDi" wraps cleanly to two lines with no truncation, arrow icon still positioned correctly, confirmed working before moving on to District 2.

v3.2.3 - 2026-08-17 - Added District 1 (TriBeCa / Battery Park City / Financial District) -- the first area added since the original three, and the first built against a real administrative boundary (NYC Community District 1, `boro_cd=101` from NYC Open Data) rather than a hand-picked bbox, per the new Manhattan-wide district plan. Mainland polygon only -- the small Ellis/Liberty/Governors Island fragments in CD1's real MultiPolygon were dropped since Joe's district map doesn't include them under "District 1."

Fetched live: real Overture buildings + building_parts pulled directly from Overture's public PMTiles (`overturemaps-extras-us-west-2` S3, release 2026-07-22.0) via a live browser session, decoded with `@mapbox/vector-tile`, not simulated. Same known PMTiles gotchas as before, checked and fixed again on this new data rather than assumed fixed for good: tile-boundary duplicate IDs (569 found, largest-fragment-wins dedup applied), all rings came out clockwise again (fixed), outbuilding/roof clutter filtered (137 features). Clipped to the real CD1 polygon by building centroid, not the fetch bbox, so neighboring future districts won't double-count edge buildings. Final counts: 1,719 buildings, 1,975 building_parts -- the largest area yet (1.16x Chelsea, the previous largest).

Streets: same `inkn-q76z` Socrata Centerline source as before, `streetwidth>=60`, clipped by segment midpoint to the same real polygon. 10 named streets (Church, Greenwich, West, Water, Pearl, Centre, Battery Pl, South End Ave, W Thames, Ave of the Americas).

Grid tilt: measured at 29.06 deg by the same weighted-circular-mean-of-wall-bearings method as the other three -- but flagged to Joe first as a real methodological concern specific to this district, not just measured and shipped blind. District 1 isn't one uniform grid the way the other three are: TriBeCa sits on the regular Commissioners' grid, but the Financial District is colonial-era Manhattan with genuinely irregular pre-grid streets. The measured 29.06 deg is likely dominated by TriBeCa's longer, straighter walls rather than representing the district as a whole. Joe reviewed and chose to use it anyway (close enough) rather than block on a more sophisticated per-subarea approach -- worth remembering if District 1's massing looks subtly off-grid in the Financial District portion specifically, since that's the predicted failure mode, not a surprise if it shows up.

Verified end-to-end with the real compute pipeline before shipping (Node harness, actual manifold-3d WASM, this district's actual data, not guessed): extrude -> union -> difference completes in 618ms (vs Chelsea's 307ms, the previous largest), 71,546 triangles, Manifold returns NoError. `audit_deploy.js`: 59/59 passing with District 1 wired into the `NEIGHBORHOODS` array.

v3.2.2 - 2026-08-15 - Investigation only, nothing shipped to index.html. Followed up on v3.2.1's "adjacent buildings sharing an exact corner vertex" hypothesis for the residual non-manifold edges — built a Node harness reproducing the app's real project/extrude/union/difference pipeline against the real per-neighborhood data, eroding every building footprint inward (via `CrossSection.offset()`) at four real values before unioning, then writing an actual binary STL through the app's own `exportSTL()` dedup logic and checking each one with `trimesh` (same tool/method as v3.2.1's original finding, not a theory).

Two real findings, and the first one overturns v3.2.1's own working hypothesis: (1) the raw indexed Manifold mesh -- BEFORE STL export -- has zero non-manifold edges at every erosion value tested, including zero erosion, for all three neighborhoods. `Manifold.difference()` returning `NoError` genuinely guarantees a manifold result (that's the library's core invariant), so "two buildings sharing an exact corner vertex" was never actually the cause -- it was an unverified guess written into v3.2.1's changelog and AUDIT.md without being checked, and this round checked it and found it wrong. The real non-manifold edges only appear after STL's indexless triangle-soup export and trimesh's own reload/re-weld pass -- a precision/quantization artifact of that specific round-trip, not a geometric coincidence in the source building data.

(2) Given that, footprint erosion does NOT reliably fix the actual (post-export) defect. It helped Hell's Kitchen a lot (114->9 non-manifold edges at 0.05m) and Chelsea somewhat (94->44 at 0.02m), but Hudson Yards moved non-monotonically across the same sweep (58/49/58/43 edges at 0.02/0.05/0.08/0.12m against a 44-edge baseline) -- no single erosion value helped all three, and the Hudson Yards numbers don't even trend cleanly with erosion depth. Shipping a footprint-accuracy tradeoff (real, if tiny, gaps between party-wall buildings) for an effect this inconsistent isn't justified, so nothing was changed in index.html. The real mechanism behind the STL-export non-manifold edges is still unknown -- worth chasing directly next time (e.g. bisecting which specific triangles/vertices trimesh's re-weld disagrees with) rather than guessing at a plausible-sounding geometric cause again. AUDIT.md's "Open" section corrected to reflect this rather than repeat the disproven hypothesis.

v3.2.1 - 2026-08-15 - Real investigation into whether the STL export is actually watertight, prompted by Joe not having slicing software to check it himself -- pulled a real exported file back and ran it through `trimesh` (an external, independent mesh library) rather than trusting this app's own claims about itself. Two real, DIFFERENT findings, not one:

(1) Deduped exact-duplicate triangles in `exportSTL()` itself (a legitimate, harmless improvement, kept from the earlier investigation) -- but verified directly that this does NOT fix watertightness by itself, correcting an earlier premature conclusion in this same investigation.

(2) The real dominant cause, found by tracing actual non-manifold edges back to their triangles: the mold bounding box was sized to EXACTLY the tightest bbox of all buildings, so a building sitting right at the site's edge has its footprint touching the mold boundary exactly -- a classic degenerate case for any CSG boolean. Fixed by giving the mold box a small (2m) margin beyond the tightest building bbox on X/Y only (deliberately NOT on Z/capHeight, where the tallest building touching the cap exactly is intentional, see that variable's own comment). `siteMinX/Y/MaxX/Y` (which drive camera framing, poché fill sizing, and cut-plane positioning) updated to match the real, now-larger mold box.

Verified end-to-end with the ACTUAL manifold-3d WASM library and real per-neighborhood data in a Node harness (not guessed, not simulated) -- and the honest result is: this fix measurably helps Hudson Yards (66 -> 51 non-manifold edges, 0.214% -> 0.166%) but makes ZERO difference for Chelsea or Hell's Kitchen (109 and 125 bad edges respectively, unchanged before/after, confirmed by pushing the margin from 2m to 10m with no further change for any neighborhood). Traced the residual to a genuinely different, unrelated cause: adjacent buildings sharing an exact corner vertex (common and structurally real for NYC party-wall rowhouses), which a mold-boundary margin cannot address -- would need a different approach (e.g. a tiny footprint erosion before union) to fix, not attempted this round. All three neighborhoods' real non-manifold rate after this fix: Hudson Yards 0.166%, Chelsea 0.151%, Hell's Kitchen 0.241% -- small, and very likely handled fine by any real slicer's own auto-repair, but this is NOT a claim that the export is now fully watertight, and it would be wrong to say so.

v3.2.0 - 2026-08-15 - Added STL export -- the first genuinely new feature since the multi-neighborhood work, not a fix. Exports whichever layer is currently showing (Buildings massing, or the computed Negative space solid once that's been run) as a real binary STL file, downloadable directly from the browser. This is the actual point of the project made literal: the negative space stops being something you only look at on screen and becomes something millable/printable, matching Joe's own CNC/fabrication practice (Sound/Relief already does CNC relief output from a different kind of data).

Binary STL (not ASCII) -- the standard virtually every slicer/CAM tool expects, and far more compact for a mesh this size (tens of thousands of triangles for a full neighborhood). Written directly via DataView (80-byte header, uint32 triangle count, 50 bytes/triangle: float32 normal + three float32 vertices + a zeroed attribute count) rather than pulling in an external STL-writer dependency, matching this project's no-framework-unless-genuinely-needed convention. New "Model scale 1:N" input (default 1:500, the real architectural-model convention) converts the app's real-world-meter geometry to physical-model millimeters at that scale -- necessary because a literal 1:1 export of a city block is meaningless for any actual desktop CNC bed or print volume.

Verified the binary format logic in an isolated Node harness before wiring it into the app (synthetic triangles -> write bytes -> parse them back with a plain DataView reader -> confirm exact float32 round-trip and correct computed normals) -- this is the actual risk area (byte offsets, stride, endianness), not the THREE.js mesh traversal itself (a standard, low-risk use of BufferAttribute/Matrix4). NOT yet live-verified against a real slicer/CAD tool opening the exported file, or against Joe's actual running browser (no live connection reached his machine this round) -- worth a real check next: export both Buildings and a computed Negative space, open each in an actual STL viewer/slicer, and confirm they load as valid, watertight (for the negative-space case, since Manifold's output should already guarantee that) solids at the expected physical scale.

v3.1.9 - 2026-08-15 - Added permanent avenue/street name labels (Joe's ask: "at least major avenues and st - 42 st, 34 st, 57 st"). One label per unique real street name in the current neighborhood's data, not one per block segment ("9 AVE" alone repeats across many short block-length rows -- labeling every occurrence would be clutter) -- for each name, finds its single longest real run across every row sharing that name and places one label at that run's real midpoint, so the label lands on a genuinely long, legible stretch rather than an arbitrary short block. Same canvas-texture Sprite technique as the existing borough labels (`makeBoroughLabel`), scaled down for site/building scale (90m world-width vs boroughs' 2000-2600m) and using the same theme-agnostic white-text-with-dark-shadow styling -- deliberately NOT reading the `--ink` CSS var the way street/borough MESH materials do, since labels (like the existing borough ones) are only rebuilt on neighborhood switch, not on theme toggle, so a CSS-var-read color would go stale the moment the user toggles theme without also switching neighborhoods. Labels are children of `streetsGroup`, so they toggle with the existing Major streets control rather than adding a new one. Title-cased from the source data's raw "W 42 ST" / "9 AVE" form (preserving short tokens like "W"/"ST"/"AVE" as-is) rather than shown all-caps, since these read at a much smaller, denser scale than the borough names. NOT yet live-verified (browser tooling this round could only reach Joe's already-running v3.1.8 instance, not a page actually serving this new code) -- worth a real check once loaded: label placement/legibility at both close and far zoom, and that they correctly hide/show with the Major streets toggle.

v3.1.8 - 2026-08-15 - The REAL root cause of the wedge/pie-slice buildings, found after v3.1.6's outbuilding/roof fix turned out to be necessary but not sufficient. Joe circled 6+ specific wedge-shaped buildings in Hell's Kitchen on a live screenshot; every geometric integrity check on the shipped v3.1.7 data came back clean (ring winding, self-intersection, degenerate rings, null heights, top-cap triangulated area vs bbox area, top-cap triangle normal orientation) -- meaning the shapes weren't corrupted once fetched, so the bug had to be upstream, in the fetch itself. Found it by noticing something that shouldn't be possible: 6 small triangular buildings, spread across 750m of longitude (multiple avenues apart), all sitting within about 30m of the exact SAME latitude. Computed the real z14 PMTiles tile-row boundary latitude for that area (via the standard tile2lat formula) and it landed almost exactly on that line. Root cause: a building spanning two adjacent map tiles gets a DIFFERENT clipped fragment under the same id in each tile (standard vector-tile behavior), and the fetch code's dedup logic kept whichever fragment it happened to see first -- sometimes a small tile-edge sliver instead of the fragment containing most of the real footprint, leaving a wedge-shaped remnant with the rest of the building simply missing from the data entirely (not hidden, not mis-rendered -- never fetched). Fixed by changing the dedup rule to always keep the LARGEST-area fragment per id across every tile fetched, for both buildings and building_parts. Re-fetched all three neighborhoods with the fix and verified directly against the same "small triangle near a tile boundary" signature that found the bug -- Hudson Yards and Hell's Kitchen: zero remaining. Chelsea: one harmless residual (a building whose top-level footprint is still an odd small shape, but it has 13 real building_parts, and the app's own rendering logic uses ONLY a building's parts when it has any -- confirmed this specific row in buildings.geojson is dead data that is never actually drawn). New counts unchanged from v3.1.6/7 (the fix changes WHICH fragment is kept per id, not the fetch bbox or count): Hudson Yards 527/458, Chelsea 1,484/693, Hell's Kitchen 1,116/284.

v3.1.7 - 2026-08-15 - Closed the streets gap flagged as a known limitation in v3.1.6. Re-fetched all three neighborhoods' major-streets data with a properly wide buffer this time (~350-400m beyond each neighborhood's building bbox, matching v3.0.26's original Hudson Yards buffer scale) rather than the too-tight one from the discarded mid-session attempt. New counts: Hudson Yards 150 segments (was 132 before v3.1.6, 81 in the discarded attempt), Chelsea 118 (was 120, 59 discarded), Hell's Kitchen 121 (was 29, 33 discarded) -- Hell's Kitchen's jump is the real fix here, now covering real streets (including Broadway, Columbus Ave, Amsterdam Ave, W57th St) all the way up through its new W59th northern edge, matching v3.1.6's expanded building coverage instead of stopping at the old ~W54th boundary. All three verified as real, correctly-filtered data (streetwidth>=60, same Socrata inkn-q76z source) before shipping, not just trusted from the fetch.

v3.1.6 - 2026-08-15 - Two real fixes from Joe's live-in-the-neighborhood feedback (he lived on 10th Ave near W55th St for 20 years and flagged Hell's Kitchen's building forms as visibly off, plus pointed out triangular "pie wedge" shapes that don't correspond to any real building).

Root cause of the wedges, found by checking real geometry rather than guessing: a handful of Overture features in the existing data were tagged `subtype: 'outbuilding'`, `class: 'roof'` -- non-primary structures like canopies and shed roofs, not real buildings. One confirmed example from Hudson Yards' own shipped v3.1.5 data: a 5.1m-tall footprint spanning ~200m in length but only a few meters wide (near the rail yards) -- extrudes into exactly the thin wedge/triangle artifact from Joe's screenshot. Fixed by excluding `subtype === 'outbuilding'` / `class === 'roof'` features at the fetch stage. Verified with a real geometric check (signed area / perimeter² "thinness" ratio) across all three neighborhoods' re-fetched data: zero large-and-thin outliers remain in the top-level `buildings.geojson` for any neighborhood (was 1 major offender + several smaller candidates in the old Hudson Yards data alone). Note: a small number of `building_part` sub-features (8 total across all three neighborhoods) still measure as thin by the same test -- these are architectural sub-components of buildings that DO have real substance (a thin roof section or connector between two volumes of the same building), not freestanding wedges, and Overture's `building_part` schema doesn't carry the same `subtype`/`class` tagging used to filter the top-level case. Left as a known minor residual, not chased further this round.

Root cause of the "geometry looks off" report at 10th Ave/W55th: verified the actual bbox of the previously-shipped Hell's Kitchen data only reached to ~West 54th St (its northern edge measured at latitude 40.7662) -- 10th Ave & 55th St sits right at or just past that edge (measured against a real anchor, the 50th St/9th Ave subway station at a confirmed 40.7634861, plus NYC's real block spacing). That block was either missing or ragged-edge-clipped, not actually rendering wrong geometry for what it did have. Refetched all three neighborhoods with meaningfully wider bounding boxes (Hell's Kitchen now genuinely reaches its real northern edge past W59th; Hudson Yards and Chelsea both widened by roughly 120m/a couple of blocks on every side, per Joe's separate ask to add "a few avenues and streets to all," not just Hell's Kitchen) -- new counts: Hudson Yards 527 buildings (was 302), Chelsea 1,484 (was 1,226), Hell's Kitchen 1,116 (was 438). Re-measured each neighborhood's real grid tilt fresh against the new, larger building samples (same v3.0.27 weighted-circular-mean method) rather than reusing the old values, since a materially different, larger sample of the same real street grid can legitimately refine the measurement: Hudson Yards 28.57° (was 28.96°), Chelsea 28.48° (was 28.55°), Hell's Kitchen 28.89° (was 28.87°, barely moved -- consistent, since that neighborhood's core sample was already large).

KNOWN LIMITATION, not fixed this round: the major-streets overlay (streets.json) was NOT re-fetched to match the new wider building bboxes -- browser tooling disconnected mid-session before a properly wide streets refetch could complete (a first attempt came back with FEWER streets than the previous version, from an accidentally too-tight buffer, and was discarded rather than shipped). Streets still reflect the OLD, narrower bboxes for all three neighborhoods -- correctly positioned and real, just not extending to match the new building coverage, so buildings near each neighborhood's new outer edge (especially Hell's Kitchen's newly-added W55th-59th blocks) will show with no nearby streets overlay until this is revisited.

v3.1.5 - 2026-08-15 - Added audit_deploy.js, matching the standard convention Joe's other tools all have -- this project had never had one. Not a generic template copy-paste: most sections are keyed to this project's own real bug history (a second, testable copy of CHANGELOG.md, per the convention), plus the universal template checks (changelog/footer/version sync, escapeHtml/escapeAttr/toast/confirmDialog present, iOS meta tags). Added confirmDialog() itself too while at it -- present per the standard shape (a custom Promise-based modal, never native confirm(), since iOS silently disables it for home-screen-installed apps) even though nothing calls it yet; built on the .confirm-overlay/.confirm-box CSS that had sat unused in this file since its very first version.

Real project-specific checks: (1) no top-level scene.add(...)/renderer.domElement.addEventListener(...) calls -- the exact v3.0.14 regression class, where either call at true top-level script scope silently kills the whole module before boot() ever runs. (2) every NEIGHBORHOODS entry has its three real data files present on disk, and no two neighborhoods share a gridRotationDeg. (3) no leftover embedded #buildingsData/#buildingPartsData/#streetsData script tags (the pre-v3.1.0 architecture this project moved away from). (4) real winding-order check on every neighborhood's actual buildings.geojson -- computes real signed area on every outer ring and confirms none are wound clockwise, the exact Chelsea/Hell's Kitchen PMTiles gotcha from v3.1.0/v3.1.1, checked against the real shipped data files, not just described in a comment. (5) nav-panel/footer vertical clearance (v3.1.2's overlap fix). (6) help modal is actually wired up (v3.1.3). (7) netlify.toml exists and is shaped correctly (v3.1.4).

Real bug caught and fixed while building the top-level-call scanner itself, before it ever shipped: the first working version tracked brace depth by walking the source char-by-char, skipping over string/comment contents -- but didn't know about regex literals, and immediately mis-parsed this very file's own escapeHtml(), whose regex (`/[&<>"']/g`) contains a literal `"` and `'` INSIDE its character class. Those got misread as real string delimiters, silently swallowing a real `{` into a bogus "string span" and throwing the whole depth count off by one for the entire rest of the file (confirmed directly: final depth came out 1, not 0, on this file's own real, valid, `node --check`-passing source -- and with that bug in place, the scanner would have silently failed to flag ANY real top-level offender anywhere past that point, a false-negative in exactly the class of bug this check exists to catch). Fixed with a standard regex-vs-division disambiguation heuristic (classify the previous significant token -- after an identifier/number/`)`/`]`/closed-string, `/` means division; otherwise, including after keywords like `return`/`typeof`, it starts a regex literal) plus character-class-aware regex scanning. Verified the fix two ways before trusting it, not just by reasoning about it: confirmed the real file's own module script now resolves to depth 0 at end-of-file (the only value valid, balanced JS can produce), then injected a genuine top-level `scene.add(...)` call directly into the real file and confirmed the check caught it, then confirmed it cleared again once the injection was reverted.

v3.1.4 - 2026-08-15 - Added netlify.toml -- was missing entirely; this project had never actually been wired for continuous deployment despite being live at axisbim.io (presumably deployed manually or via drag-and-drop up to now). Matches the standard convention across Joe's tool suite: `publish = "."` (no build step, single index.html plus the data/ folder), baseline security headers (X-Frame-Options, X-Content-Type-Options, Referrer-Policy) on everything, and a no-cache Cache-Control on index.html specifically so a version bump is never served stale from Netlify's CDN. data/*.geojson and streets.json get no special caching rule -- default static-asset caching is fine for them, and they only change when a neighborhood is actually added, not on every deploy.

v3.1.3 - 2026-08-15 - Help documentation, three-part per Joe's ask: (1) in-app help modal, a new "?" button next to the theme toggle (matching the convention MASSING already established for Joe's tool suite) -- covers Neighborhood/Compute/Buildings-vs-Negative-space, the cutaway sliders + poché + drag handles, N/S/E/W/Plan/Home navigation + compass, and Manhattan context/Major streets, with links out to README.md/EXTENDED.md for more. Built on the existing (until now unused) `.confirm-overlay`/`.confirm-box` modal pattern already in the template, widened and made scrollable for real content rather than a one-line confirm prompt; closes on the X button, a backdrop click, or Escape. (2) README.md added -- kept short (what it is, how to run it, links out) per the project's own established README convention (short prose, no emoji, no badges). (3) EXTENDED.md added -- the longer user/developer guide AUDIT.md was never meant to be (AUDIT.md stays the technical-architecture-for-whoever's-editing-the-code doc): a full feature walkthrough in plain language, a step-by-step "how to add a new neighborhood" workflow (the same process Hudson Yards/Chelsea/Hell's Kitchen already went through, written up as a repeatable procedure now that there's a third data point to generalize from), and a troubleshooting section for the handful of symptoms that have recurred across this project's history.

v3.1.2 - 2026-08-15 - Four small fixes/additions from Joe's screenshot + nitpick list. (1) Neighborhood picker switched from a row of secondary buttons to a single `<select>` dropdown (`initNeighborhoodPicker()`/`switchNeighborhood()` reworked accordingly) -- Joe's own question ("should we add a pull down?") flagging that one button per neighborhood won't scale past a handful without wrapping into a cluttered multi-row grid; a pull-down stays a single control no matter how many neighborhoods get added. (2) Fixed the nav panel visually overlapping the footer credit line (Joe's screenshot) -- both were anchored to the same bottom-right corner with only 6px of nominal separation (navPanel bottom:16px vs #footVersion bottom:10px), so the footer text rendered through the nav panel's own bottom edge. Lifted #navPanel to bottom:calc(46px + safe-area) instead, clearing real room for the footer beneath it rather than fighting over the same corner. (3) Default theme changed from light to Blueprint (dark) on a fresh visit, and the Major streets overlay now defaults to visible (`showStreets` true) -- both still respect an explicit prior user choice (localStorage keeps persisting real clicks either way), only the previously-unset defaults changed.
