(4) Added a true top-down Plan view (new "P" button in the nav grid, `setOrthogonalView('plan')`) -- N/S/E/W give flat elevations but none of them was ever actually looking straight down. Positions the orthographic camera directly above the target (offset purely along +Z, this scene's own up axis). First draft added a horizontal up-vector swap (0,1,0) on the theory that OrbitControls' final camera orientation would hit a degenerate lookAt case with up parallel to a straight-down view direction -- turned out to be an untested guess, and live-testing it directly (before shipping, not after) against a real running scene disproved it: built an isolated throwaway OrthographicCamera + OrbitControls instance, positioned it at the exact same pole with the scene's ORDINARY up=(0,0,1) left untouched, and read back a clean result -- forward vector (0,0,-1) (straight down, correct), local screen-up mapping to world (0,1,0) (north-up, correct), zero special-casing needed. A follow-up test nudging the camera slightly off that pole (simulating a small orbit drag away from Plan) also came back stable, no NaN or gimbal-lock blowup. Shipped the simpler version -- orthoCamera.up never changes at all, for Plan or any other direction -- and corrected the code comment + this entry to describe what was actually tested rather than the discarded theory.


v3.1.1 - 2026-08-15 - Two items: real per-neighborhood streets (last KNOWN LIMITATION from v3.1.0), and a third neighborhood, Hell's Kitchen. (1) Major streets were still the fixed ~1.1km-radius-around-Hudson-Yards set from v3.0.26, shown misleadingly under every neighborhood via re-projection. Reworked buildStreetsLayer() to be async and fetch data/<neighborhoodId>/streets.json (same live-fetch pattern loadData() already uses for buildings/building_parts), instead of reading a single shared embedded #streetsData <script> tag (now removed). loadData() awaits it before syncing the toggle. Filtered real NYC Street Centerline data (Socrata inkn-q76z, streetwidth>=60, same source/filter as v3.0.26) per neighborhood from MASSING's own cached citywide copy: Hudson Yards (132 segments, same real streets as before, now in its own file), Chelsea (120 segments, real -- 8th/9th/10th/11th Ave, Ave of the Americas, W30th/34th St), Hell's Kitchen (29 segments, matching its smaller footprint -- 9th/10th/11th Ave, W42nd St). (2) Added Hell's Kitchen as a third neighborhood (438 buildings, 82 building_parts), core bbox W44th-W50th St between 9th-10th Ave (chosen to avoid overlapping Hudson Yards' own northern edge). Same pipeline as Chelsea: fetched real Overture building/building_part data from the 2026-07-22.0 PMTiles release via pmtiles.js + @mapbox/vector-tile decoding, hit the same clockwise-ring-winding gotcha Chelsea's extraction found (100% of rings CW, silently zero-area under Manifold's CrossSection) and fixed it the same way (shoelace-sign check + reversal). Grid tilt independently measured via the same v3.0.27 weighted-circular-mean-of-wall-bearings method: 28.87 degrees -- close to Hudson Yards' 28.96 and Chelsea's 28.55, as expected for the same underlying Manhattan grid, but measured fresh from Hell's Kitchen's own real building data, not assumed or copied. Data extraction and download happened via a connected browser session (same live-fetch technique as Chelsea); Joe uploaded the resulting buildings.geojson/building_parts.geojson directly to chat, verified matching the generated data before being placed in data/hells-kitchen/.
KNOWN LIMITATION resolved: streets are now real per-neighborhood data, not a misleading shared set. No known major limitations remain open.

v3.1.0 - 2026-08-15 - Joe's chosen strategy for Manhattan-scale expansion, after clarifying it's unrelated to his own PLUTO Massing tool (only its search/browse UX pattern was ever borrowed, never its code or live-fetch-any-area approach): build out Manhattan neighborhood-by-neighborhood, each one individually fetched, winding-corrected, and grid-tilt-measured before being added as a selectable entry -- not a live system that geocodes/fetches/computes any arbitrary area on demand with no verification step. Proved the full pipeline first on real Chelsea Overture data (1,226 buildings, 556 building_parts, bbox -74.008,40.742,-73.996,40.751, fetched from Overture's 2026-07-22.0 PMTiles archive via pmtiles.js + vector-tile decoding since the STAC catalog was down that day) before writing any app code. That proof run hit and resolved a real, separate root cause worth its own note: @mapbox/vector-tile's toGeoJSON() output every single Chelsea ring (1226/1226) as clockwise, and Manifold's CrossSection under its default Positive fill rule treats CW rings as zero-area -- every extrude() silently returned InvalidConstruction status, which read as a 100+ second hang rather than the fast, clear failure it actually was. Fixed by detecting winding via shoelace signed-area sign and reversing any CW ring before building the CrossSection; with that fixed, a Node.js harness (manifold-3d via npm) confirmed the full pipeline for all 1,226 buildings completes in 0.6s -- Manifold's own scaling was never the problem.
Architecture change to support switching: data is no longer embedded in the HTML (the <script type="application/json"> buildingsData/buildingPartsData tags are gone) -- loadData(neighborhoodId) now fetches data/<id>/buildings.geojson and data/<id>/building_parts.geojson live. This means plain double-clicked file:// no longer works at all (not even the old embedded-data workaround); the app now REQUIRES being served over http(s), same requirement Manifold's wasm-from-unpkg already had. GRID_ROTATION_DEG changed from a hardcoded const to a mutable value set by setGridRotation() per neighborhood (each one's real tilt independently measured via the same v3.0.27 weighted-circular-mean method -- Hudson Yards 28.96°, Chelsea 28.55°, genuinely different, not reused). Added a NEIGHBORHOODS config array and a generated button row (Neighborhood picker) in the sidebar; switching calls loadData() again, which now properly tears down and disposes the PREVIOUS neighborhood's solidGroup/negativeMesh/capFillGroups/cut-line meshes and resets all UI state (stats placeholders, hidden cutaway sliders, re-enabled compute button) before loading the new one -- this didn't exist before since loadData() only ever ran once at boot. boroughsGroup/streetsGroup (the Manhattan outline + major-streets overlays) also now rebuild on every switch rather than once, since both are re-projected relative to the CURRENT projection origin, which changes per neighborhood -- building them only once would leave them permanently misaligned relative to any neighborhood switched to after the first. KNOWN LIMITATION, not fixed here: the streets overlay content is still the fixed ~1.1km-around-Hudson-Yards dataset from v3.0.26, correctly repositioned relative to whichever neighborhood is active but not yet real per-neighborhood street data.
Live-verified precision on both neighborhoods, in both switch directions, with real numbers (not just visual inspection, per Joe's explicit ask that this be treated as a priority correctness rule): after switching Chelsea (computed) back to Hudson Yards, confirmed via window.__NS that negativeMesh/capFillGroups were properly null (no leaked Chelsea geometry), then ran a fresh compute and checked the actual cutaway slider min/max attributes and negativeMesh's own THREE.Box3 bounds against Hudson Yards' real geometry: height cap 395m, triangle count 13,802, X range -448.0..690.6, Y range -505.0..568.4 -- all matching the neighborhood's genuine dimensions, zero cross-contamination from Chelsea. Switched back to Chelsea and confirmed the reverse: height cap 123.0m (Chelsea's real, much-lower tallest building), triangle count 39,278 (matching the earlier proof-of-concept run exactly), correctly distinct X/Y ranges. Two neighborhoods shipped: Hudson Yards (497 buildings, default) and Chelsea (1,226 buildings).
v3.0.42 - 2026-08-14 - Joe pointed out the N/S/E/W nav buttons were isometric-style perspective views, not true flat 2D elevations -- asked for real orthogonal views. Confirmed the diagnosis: setOrthogonalView() just repositioned the app's single THREE.PerspectiveCamera to look along an axis, so buildings still shrank with distance from the camera (genuine perspective foreshortening, however subtle at this FOV/distance) -- nothing like an actual architectural elevation/plan drawing, where apparent size is independent of viewer distance. There's no FOV or camera-distance trick that removes perspective foreshortening; it requires a genuine parallel (orthographic) projection. Added a second camera (orthoCamera, a real THREE.OrthographicCamera) plus a new activeCamera indirection used throughout the render loop, resize handler, compass, and drag-handle raycasting -- N/S/E/W now switch activeCamera to orthoCamera (reassigning the SAME OrbitControls instance's .object property rather than maintaining two separate controls instances with competing mouse/touch listeners -- a supported, well-established three.js pattern also used in three.js's own multi-camera examples), framed via a new currentTargetAndSpan() (parallel to the old currentTargetAndDistance(), but returning a real-world characteristic size instead of reusing whatever distance the perspective camera happened to be parked at -- an orthographic frustum's half-height needs an actual model size, not a leftover perspective zoom level) and positioned with ZERO vertical lift (the old version deliberately added an 18% lift so a purely edge-on perspective view didn't read as a flat line; that workaround doesn't apply here, since reading perfectly flat and perpendicular to the labeled axis is the entire point of a true elevation). Home/Recenter switches back to the perspective camera. Manhattan-context toggle forces a return to perspective too if triggered while an N/S/E/W flat view was active, since repositioning `camera` directly (as it already does) would otherwise silently not show up until Home was also pressed. Given the container tools were mid-outage when this was designed, the core projection mechanism was fully prototyped and confirmed FIRST directly against Joe's real running v3.0.41 session (built a second camera + controls live, intercepted renderer.render to force it through, confirmed visually that same-height buildings read the same height regardless of distance) before a single line went into the file. Once the container recovered and the real change was written, re-verified the actual new logic end-to-end the same way -- this time porting the exact new currentTargetAndSpan()/setOrthogonalView() code, reading the REAL post-compute site bounds directly off the live sliders (span=1,138m, matching the real X/Y extent) rather than guessed values, and confirmed clean, correctly-oriented, well-framed flat elevations in all 4 directions (N/S mirror each other along Y as expected, E/W mirror along X), plus a clean return to the normal perspective 3/4 view afterward. The drag-handle raycasting switch to activeCamera (so the cutaway handles stay draggable from within a flat elevation view too) uses the same standard THREE.Raycaster.setFromCamera() API for both camera types and was not separately live-tested -- a straightforward substitution, lower risk than the camera-switching mechanism itself, but worth a real check next time the handles come up.

v3.0.41 - 2026-08-14 - Joe reported v3.0.40's Flip X/Y "not moving in the right direction." Live-tested v3.0.40 directly and confirmed: toggling Flip X still carved away the SAME positive-X/positive-Y corner every time, just a different amount at a given slider position -- v3.0.40's mirror-the-slider-value approach could only ever change how much got cut, never WHICH corner, since it never touched the clip plane's actual direction. Real fix this time: xClipPlane/yClipPlane's own .normal genuinely flips now (1 vs -1 on the relevant axis), with .constant re-derived to match (xPlaneConstant(t) = flip ? -t : t, the correct sign so "keep x<=t" becomes "keep x>=t" at the exact same natural position t). Introduced xThreshold/yThreshold as a new single source of truth for "where the cut sits" -- a natural, always-real-world value completely independent of flip state, matching exactly what the slider/label/poché quad/drag handle show -- with xClipPlane/yClipPlane's actual normal+constant, their negated copies (xClipPlaneNeg/yClipPlaneNeg), and their epsilon-nudged stencil copies (xClipPlaneStencil/yClipPlaneStencil) all now DERIVED from threshold+flip inside syncCapFillPlanes() rather than written to directly from half a dozen scattered call sites. setXCutaway/setYCutaway/resetCutaway/the unit-toggle label refresh/the poché quad position/the 3D drag handle position all switched from reading xClipPlane.constant directly to reading xThreshold (a real bug caught mid-implementation: refreshUnitLabels(), the Metric/Imperial toggle's label refresh, still read xClipPlane.constant directly -- would have displayed a sign-flipped, wrong number whenever units were toggled while a flip was active, if not caught). Toggling Flip X/Y now keeps the cut position exactly where it was and swaps which side of it is solid vs excavated, in place -- the actually-intended behavior. Live-verified the core mechanism directly (not just reasoned about): patched the real xClipPlane object on Joe's own running session via window.__NS at a fixed natural threshold of 300m, unflipped vs flipped -- unflipped showed the box nearly SEALED (matches the existing, already-proven convention), flipped at the exact same 300m threshold showed it nearly HOLLOW instead, the genuine opposite-corner result v3.0.40 never achieved.

v3.0.40 - 2026-08-14 - Joe's screenshot: at near-minimum X/Y cutaway values (-448M/-505M), the octant cut hollowed out almost the ENTIRE mold box, leaving just its own far-side shell walls standing -- two huge flat planes dominating the view, barely any excavated interior actually visible. Asked to flip the cut direction for X and Y so the excavation happens on the opposite side instead. Added "Flip X"/"Flip Y" toggle buttons next to their respective sliders. Rather than flipping xClipPlane/yClipPlane's own normal -- which the main clipIntersection removal test, the negated-plane restriction system shared by BOTH poché and cut-lines, the stencil epsilon-bias sign, the poché quad's own rotation direction, and the drag-handle raycasting axis all read directly, meaning a normal-flip would need every one of those correctly re-derived, real surgery with a lot of ways to get one subtly backwards -- this mirrors the SLIDER's raw value around the site's own axis midpoint ((siteMinX+siteMaxX)-raw) before ever calling setXCutaway(), which is the only place flip state gets consulted. Every other system (plane objects, negated planes, stencil epsilon planes, poché quads, cut-line cross-section, 3D drag handles) stays completely unaware flipping exists -- they only ever see the final real-world constant, so all of them stay correct with zero changes. The 3D drag handles are deliberately NOT mirrored, since they already pass a real world-space position from where you dragged; mirroring that would move the plane somewhere other than where you dragged to. "Reset cutaway" also stays unaffected by design -- it sets the literal x=0/y=0/z=0 position directly, not through the slider's mirrored path, so Reset always returns to the same real physical position regardless of flip state. Live-verified the actual downstream rendering effect (not just the arithmetic) on Joe's real running v3.0.39 session: computed the exact mirrored values for his reported -448/-505 by hand, set them directly through the page's own real slider-input event chain, and confirmed the box now reads mostly SEALED (thin sliver corner exposed) -- the exact opposite of his screenshot's mostly-HOLLOW result, which is precisely what flipping those same slider positions should produce.

v3.0.39 - 2026-08-14 - Joe's screenshot: pressing the N or E nav buttons while zoomed out to Manhattan context showed nothing but two huge close-up translucent shell walls, with the real Manhattan outline still faintly visible far behind them. Root cause, found via code review (Claude-in-Chrome was disconnected this round, see note below): `setOrthogonalView()` (what N/S/E/W call) pivots around whatever `currentTargetAndDistance()` returns. That function correctly reused the CURRENT camera-to-target distance (large, borough-scale, when zoomed out) but ALWAYS recomputed the target as the tiny SITE's own centroid, regardless of whether Manhattan context was active -- never the borough's own centroid that `zoomToBoroughContext()` actually frames the view around. A large borough-scale distance combined with a sudden jump to a much closer, different (tiny-site) target put the camera right up against, or effectively inside, the mold box's own translucent shell walls -- consistent with the screenshot (huge walls filling the frame, real Manhattan geometry still visible far in the background since it wasn't hidden, just dwarfed by the now much-too-close shell). Fixed by making `currentTargetAndDistance()` check the `showBoroughs` flag and, when true, compute the SAME Box3-center-of-boroughsGroup that `zoomToBoroughContext()` itself uses as the pivot -- so N/S/E/W now orbit around whichever thing is actually on screen (borough or site) instead of always the site regardless of view mode. IMPORTANT CAVEAT: Claude in Chrome's browser connection dropped mid-session and could not be re-established before this fix needed to ship -- this is the first fix in the v3.0.31-39 arc NOT live-verified against Joe's actual running page before delivery. Diagnosed purely from the screenshot plus direct code reading of `setOrthogonalView`/`currentTargetAndDistance`/`zoomToBoroughContext`, and the syntax was checked, but the fix itself has not been exercised in a real browser. Needs a real live check (press N and E with Manhattan context on, at a few different zoom/cutaway states) before this can be considered confirmed, not just theorized.
UPDATE same day: connection came back. Live-verified directly on Joe's running v3.0.39 -- computed negative space, toggled Manhattan context on, pressed N (correctly framed the whole island from the north, buildings tiny and visible near the top, no walls) and E (correct elevation-style view from the east). Confirmed fixed, not just theorized.

v3.0.38 - 2026-08-14 - Joe's screenshot after v3.0.37 shipped: X cutaway showed correct red poché, but Y and the height cut (Z) showed the plain translucent shell instead -- no poché at all. Root cause, confirmed conclusively live (not theorized): three.js splits its render list into two hard-separated passes -- ALL opaque objects render first, THEN all transparent objects, as two entirely separate batches. renderOrder only sorts objects WITHIN each of those two lists; it has no effect on ordering ACROSS them (checked directly against three.js's own render-list source, not assumed from behavior). The 6 stencil-write meshes used for each axis's parity test are colorWrite:false but otherwise plain opaque materials (transparent was never set), while the 3 poché quads are transparent:true (needed for their 0.4-opacity look since v3.0.29). That meant ALL SIX stencil-parity writes (X, Y, AND Z) always completed first, bundled into one opaque batch -- only then did the 3 quads render, in sequence. X's quad (rendering first among the three, at its own renderOrder 1.1) read the just-written parity correctly, but its onAfterRender handler calls renderer.clearStencil(), which wipes WebGL's ENTIRE shared 8-bit stencil buffer -- there's no way to clear just one axis's share of it. By the time Y's quad rendered next, it was reading an all-zero buffer, and NotEqualStencilFunc(ref=0) can never pass against an all-zero buffer, so Y drew nothing. Z, rendering last, was cleared a second time over by the same mechanism before it ever got a turn. Confirmed directly on Joe's own running session before writing any fix: patched X's onAfterRender to a no-op via window.__NS with nothing else changed, and Y's poché immediately started rendering correctly -- proof the quad, its material, and its position were never the problem, only the cross-pass clear timing. Fixed by setting transparent:true on the 6 stencil-write materials too (free, since colorWrite:false already means they draw no visible pixels either way) -- this moves them into the SAME transparent render list as the quads, where a single per-list renderOrder sort now produces the actually-intended atomic sequence: X-write(1) -> X-quad read+clear(1.1) -> Y-write(2) -> Y-quad read+clear(2.1) -> Z-write(3) -> Z-quad read+clear(3.1), each axis's clear now landing exactly between its own read and the next axis's write instead of all three writes bunching up before any read happens. Live-verified the actual fix (not just the diagnosis) by patching the same materials directly on Joe's running session and confirming Y's poché correctly turned red; then re-verified with a real compute() + all 3 sliders simultaneously at nonzero, non-extreme values (X=-258, Y=-166, Z=100, none at any axis's own min/max) -- all three walls AND the floor showed correct red poché together, plus the v3.0.37 cut-line boundaries tracing correctly around every building on all three cut planes at once.

v3.0.37 - 2026-08-14 - Joe asked to tackle poché's missing boundary line and the long-standing cut-line "scattered dots" symptom (noted since v3.0.9, never investigated) together. Both turned out to be the same root cause: the cut-line highlight used THREE.EdgesGeometry, which only extracts the mesh's real hard EDGES (corners, setbacks -- anywhere two non-coplanar faces meet at a sharp angle). A flat wall has no such edges running through its own interior, so wherever a cut plane sliced through open flat material, EdgesGeometry had nothing real to find near the clipping band except a few incidental internal triangulation seams -- scattered points, not a continuous line. That's a structural mismatch, not a tolerance to tune: edge-extraction can never produce a plane's true cross-section. Replaced it with an exact triangle/plane intersection (standard clip: for each triangle edge, if its endpoints sit on opposite sides of the plane, linearly interpolate the exact crossing point; a sliced triangle always yields exactly 0 or 2 such points), computed fresh at the current slider position rather than fixed once and re-clipped. Removed the old thin clipping-band planes (CUT_LINE_HALF_THICKNESS, zBandLower/Upper etc.) entirely -- no longer needed since the new geometry sits exactly on the cut plane. This cross-section, restricted to the removed octant via the same negated planes already shared with the poché stencil system, directly resolves the separate poché-boundary backlog item too -- it IS the poché fill's true edge, no second implementation needed. Live-verified the core fix against the real running Hudson Yards mesh via the window.__NS debug hook (pulled the live geometry and cut planes out of Joe's actual running session, ran the new algorithm against it directly in the browser console, rendered the result into the live scene): clean continuous building-footprint loops at a test height, vs. the handful of stray dots the old method produced there. Caught and fixed two problems of my own before shipping, neither from Joe: (1) an initial version (JS-array push() per crossing point) Node-timed at ~9.8ms for a full 3-plane recompute at this model's ~13,800 triangles -- too much of a 16.7ms frame budget to share with actual rendering during a drag; rewrote to skip any triangle with all 3 vertices on one side before doing any interpolation work, and to write directly into a pre-allocated, reused Float32Array (sized once per mesh, paired with geometry.setDrawRange()) instead of a dynamically-grown array -- re-timed at ~2.3ms, over 4x faster. (2) Height Cut=0 (the cavity's own genuine floor -- confirmed live, ~31% of all vertices sit at exactly z=0) hit the exact same exact-coincidence degeneracy the v3.0.36 poché stencil epsilon fix was built to solve, but for the NEW cross-section computation: every triangle there is either fully on one side or exactly coincident, never a strict sign change, so the raw-plane cross-section found zero segments where the real answer is thousands. Rather than build a second epsilon mechanism, reused the SAME already-existing, already-synced sectionPlaneStencil/xClipPlaneStencil/yClipPlaneStencil epsilon-biased planes for the cross-section too -- live-confirmed against the real mesh (0 segments with the raw plane vs. 4,367 with the existing 1cm bias, at the exact same z=0 case). That required fixing a subtle ordering bug: buildCutLineMeshes() previously ran BEFORE siteMinX/Y/siteCapHeight were even set (so the epsilon planes it would depend on didn't have valid values yet at that point) -- moved it to after buildCapFillGroups(); and the 4 slider-drag call sites all called updateCutLinePlanes() BEFORE syncCapFillPlanes(), meaning the cross-section would always be reading last frame's epsilon bias, not the current one -- swapped the order at all 4. Also bumped APP_VERSION, which the file that reached the live server had initially been missing (footer still read v3.0.36 after the code fix landed, since the version constant itself hadn't been touched) -- fixed to keep the footer honest about what's actually running.

v3.0.36 - 2026-08-14 - Found the actual root cause of the poché-not-red bug Joe had reported across several rounds (v3.0.29/33/34/35 all touched symptoms of this without finding it). STENCIL_EPSILON (a tiny offset applied only to the stencil parity test's plane, to avoid it sitting exactly coincident with real geometry) was unconditionally ADDED to the plane constant -- correct near a MINIMUM extreme (nudges the test position further INTO the site, where real solid exists), but exactly backwards near a MAXIMUM extreme: at Y's true maximum, adding epsilon pushed the test position past every real building coordinate on that axis, into genuinely empty space beyond the whole site, where the parity count can only ever read "no solid found." Confirmed by bypassing the stencil test entirely (AlwaysStencilFunc) and finding the quad WAS correctly positioned and sized -- only the coincidence-avoidance offset itself was wrong. Fixed by biasing the epsilon toward whichever side of the site's own center (siteMin+siteMax)/2 the current slider value sits on, so the test position never exits the real geometric range on any axis, for any of X/Y/Z. With the real mechanism fixed rather than padded around, removed the DEAD_ZONE_MARGIN padding entirely and restored the true, unpadded slider range on all three axes -- live-verified all 6 true extremes individually (X min/max, Y min/max, Z=0/max) with a real camera and a clean bypass-vs-normal comparison for each, not just the one that was previously broken. Also fixed two earlier live-testing false leads along the way: an automated pixel-color-detection test script had too strict a threshold for how the translucent poché blends against different backgrounds, producing false "broken" readings on cases that were actually fine; and a straight-down-the-axis camera angle was viewing a wall exactly edge-on, reading as invisible for a purely geometric reason unrelated to any bug.

v3.0.35 - 2026-08-14 - Joe's explicit ask: no padding on any of the 3 cutaway sliders, true 0/extreme values reachable, and poché red on all 3 axes at those positions. Removed the DEAD_ZONE_MARGIN padding on all sliders per the request, but live-testing (with a rigorous, camera-verified pass, not just screenshots) found a real, confirmed, reproducible failure: Y's true maximum genuinely broke the poché stencil test even with the reorder fix from v3.0.34 in place. Root cause not yet found this round -- tried bumping STENCIL_EPSILON manually to 2m with no visible change, ruling out "epsilon too small" as the explanation. Given a confirmed failure and not wanting to ship something broken at a reachable slider position, reverted to the same DEAD_ZONE_MARGIN padding already proven reliable, and reported the finding plainly rather than claim it was fixed. (Superseded one version later in v3.0.36, which found and fixed the actual mechanism.)

v3.0.34 - 2026-08-14 - Joe reproduced a real, deterministic bug: with HEIGHT CUT at its own extreme (0, the real floor -- where virtually every building's base ring in the whole site sits simultaneously), X and Y's poché stencil tests silently failed, even though neither logically depends on Z's value. Live-verified directly: X's poché broke at height=0, came back correctly at height=150, broke again reverting to 0, with X/Y values held constant throughout -- ruling out anything except Z's own value as the trigger. Root cause: Z's own dense stencil pass (an enormous number of increment/decrement operations concentrated at one screen-space region, since nearly every building's base edge crosses that one plane at once) was leaving the shared 8-bit stencil buffer in a state its own clearStencil() call didn't fully reset before the NEXT axis's pass began reading it. Fixed by reordering the 3 stencil/quad passes so X and Y run first and complete cleanly before Z's problematic pass ever touches the buffer (previously Z ran first). Live-verified directly on Joe's session at his exact reported height=0 condition before shipping: X poché correctly red, then Y poché correctly red (moved X away first to isolate it), both at Height Cut=0.

v3.0.33 - 2026-08-14 - Joe reported the negative-space cutaway defaults looked wrong -- X/Y cutaway showing -442m/-499m on load, asked for 0 on all three axes ("Z can be 0"). Traced to resetCutaway()'s old per-axis default formulas: height defaulted to min(150, capHeight*0.4), X/Y defaulted to the midpoint of the site's real bounds -- none of which produce 0. Changed all three to a flat 0 default (the real coordinate origin, roughly the Hudson Yards buildings' own centroid), comfortably inside the padded slider range on every axis so no clamping was needed. Also live-tested (per Joe's specific ask) whether poché rendered correctly red at both his originally-reported extreme values and the new 0/0/0 default -- confirmed correct in both cases at the time, though the deeper mechanism behind an intermittent version of this same complaint wasn't actually found until v3.0.36.

v3.0.32 - 2026-08-14 - Joe's screenshot (referencing 4 hand-drawn reference images: real Hudson Yards blue massing, the same forms inside a labeled x/y/z-axis mold box, the boolean-subtracted "void negative form" result as one coherent translucent sealed volume, and a cutting plane sectioning through it) showed two problems with v3.0.31's newly-restored shell: (1) the X/Y cutting planes created a thin, nearly-opaque volume that fully blocked S/W nav views (a straight-on view of a translucent wall fills the ENTIRE frame edge-to-edge, with no open sky/context to break up the tint the way an oblique angle has, so even 0.5 opacity read as genuinely blocking); (2) poché showed correctly on Z but wasn't visibly red on X/Y. Root cause of (2): render order -- negativeMesh (the translucent shell) was drawing AFTER poché (renderOrder 10 vs poché's 1-3.1), so the shell's own translucent gray was compositing ON TOP of the red poché fill, visually washing it out; it was rendering correctly, just muted underneath the shell. Fixed both: dropped shell opacity from 0.5 to 0.15 (much lighter, matching how faint the shell reads in Joe's own reference images), and moved negativeMesh's renderOrder from 10 to 0 (before poché) so poché composites last and stays vividly visible. Live-verified both fixes directly on Joe's running session, specifically checking S and W nav views (the ones reported blocked) before shipping.

v3.0.31 - 2026-08-14 - Joe's four reference images made clear that v3.0.30's "delete the mold box's outer shell" fix solved the wrong problem: the negative space IS meant to be the whole "mold minus buildings" solid, shell included -- shown as ONE coherent, ghostly translucent volume with the cutting planes slicing through it, not a shell-free field of disconnected void shapes. Reverted v3.0.30's shell-filtering triangle loop entirely (back to the full, unfiltered watertight geometry for negativeMesh), and instead fixed the ORIGINAL complaint (opaque walls blocking views) the way it should have been fixed the first time: made the whole negMat translucent (transparent:true, opacity 0.5) so you can see straight through the shell from any angle without deleting the concept it represents. Simplified buildCapFillGroups' call site now that cleanedGeo is watertight again (no more separate watertightGeo copy needed for the poché stencil pass). Live-verified the translucency itself rendered cleanly (no artifacts) directly on Joe's session before shipping, though the shell-restoration half of this fix could only be confirmed once Joe loaded the actual updated file, since it required the real geometry pipeline to rerun.

v3.0.30 - 2026-08-14 - Joe's screenshot showed two huge dark opaque walls dominating the negative-space cutaway view, annotated "needs to disappear," plus reported S/E nav views were completely blocked by them. Root cause (found by inspecting negativeMesh live via window.__NS, not guessing): computeNegativeSpace() builds the whole solid as Manifold.cube(siteBox) minus buildings -- a single sealed 6-sided box with buildings subtracted out. The box's own static outer boundary faces are ALWAYS present except wherever the active octant cutaway currently happens to remove them, completely separate from the poché cut-plane overlay and from real building-void geometry. Fixed by filtering them out at the geometry level: since Manifold.cube() is a genuinely closed prism, "box minus buildings" can only ever REMOVE material from those 6 known boundary planes, never add a new face elsewhere -- so any triangle lying exactly on one of the 6 box boundary planes (minX/maxX/minY/maxY/z=0/z=capHeight) is necessarily shell, never a real building void, and got discarded from the rendered/exported geometry. Had to keep a separate, genuinely unfiltered (watertight) copy of the geometry specifically for the poché stencil-parity test, since that technique requires a sealed solid to correctly count inside/outside -- feeding it the now-intentionally-non-watertight filtered geometry would give wrong results near the site's edges. Live-verified directly in Joe's running session before shipping (patched negativeMesh.geometry in-browser, confirmed 2,191 of 13,802 triangles were shell and got removed) -- screenshotted the default oblique view AND both S/E nav buttons, confirmed completely open/unblocked from every angle. (Superseded one version later in v3.0.31, once Joe's reference images clarified the shell was actually supposed to stay, just be translucent.)

v3.0.29 - 2026-08-14 - Joe's screenshot showed the poché cut-plane fill as solid opaque near-black walls with visible hatching/aliasing, annotated "remove," referencing Modulor Massing's own clean translucent section-plane tool as the standard to match. Offered 3 options (remove poché entirely / rework as a translucent plane / full single-plane rearchitecture); Joe picked the middle one, explicitly because "this is about negative space" -- unlike Modulor Massing, showing the void's own cut boundary IS the whole point here, so poché itself needed to stay, just reworked. Changed the fill from opaque near-black to translucent (opacity 0.4) accent-red, matching both the existing red cut-line convention AND Modulor Massing's reference plane -- this also genuinely helps the long-standing hatching artifact (v3.0.8/v3.0.9), since a translucent surface blends with what's behind it instead of showing a stark opaque silhouette. Made the poché color theme-reactive (tracks the live --accent CSS var, refreshed on every theme toggle) matching the pattern already established for the borough/street overlays. Live-verified by patching the running scene's poché materials directly on Joe's session before shipping.

v3.0.28 - 2026-08-13 - Follow-up to v3.0.26: Joe asked "where are the streets?" -- confirmed via direct inspection they WERE rendering (visible from directly overhead, right where the real grid should be), but as flat 2D ribbons they had near-zero screen coverage from the app's normal oblique camera angle (a flat quad viewed edge-on), the same "paper-thin geometry disappears at grazing angles" problem this project has hit before (ground plane, borough plate). Fixed by extruding the ribbons to a real 4m height (top face + two side walls per segment, no end caps -- thin enough that missing caps don't read as gaps), giving them a genuine vertical face visible from any angle, not just top-down. Live-verified by reconstructing the extruded geometry directly from the already-built flat ribbon's own vertex data (no need to re-run project()) and confirming visibly at Joe's actual default oblique view before shipping.

v3.0.27 - 2026-08-13 - Joe pointed out the excavated "mold" box read diagonally against the real Manhattan street grid in a screenshot, and asked to grid-align the whole cutaway system to match -- explicitly chose the riskier full-rotation option over the simpler "just rotate the camera" alternative when asked. Measured the REAL grid tilt directly from the embedded building data (weighted circular mean of wall-segment bearings, favoring longer/more confident walls, not assumed or guessed): 28.96 degrees clockwise from true north, matching the well-documented ~29 degree Manhattan grid tilt almost exactly. Rather than reworking the entire cutaway/poché/cutline/handle system (every part of it assumes axis-aligned X/Y planes, hard-won through the v3.0.3-v3.0.11 debugging arc) to operate in a rotated coordinate frame, baked the rotation directly into project(lon,lat) itself -- every point in the scene (buildings, borough outlines, streets, everything) comes out of project() already grid-aligned, so the ENTIRE downstream pipeline needed zero changes; computeNegativeSpace's own site bounding box is derived straight from project() output, so it automatically became tight and grid-aligned with no extra work. Only updateCompass() needed a correction, since local +Y stopped being true north (now offset by the grid angle) -- corrected so the needle still correctly points at real true north, the same convention a real architectural site plan uses (drawn oriented to its own block grid, with a separate north arrow showing true north's actual offset). Live-verified via a plan-view screenshot showing the excavated mold's edges running exactly parallel to the real street grid.

v3.0.26 - 2026-08-13 - Major streets overlay, the last item on the original 4-item backlog. Real NYC Street Centerline data (Socrata resource inkn-q76z, streetwidth>=60), same source and filter [[pluto-massing]] already established for this exact feature. data.cityofnewyork.us isn't reachable from this project's own build sandbox (not on the network allowlist) -- fetched live via a connected browser session instead, reading it out of MASSING's own cached localStorage copy (it already fetches+caches this citywide dataset) rather than hitting the live API fresh. Discovered the javascript_tool return-value transfer channel truncates around ~1-1.5KB partway through debugging a "why is my data missing" issue -- worked around by writing the JSON into the target page's own DOM and reading it back via get_page_text, which has no comparable size limit. Trimmed the citywide dataset (6,497 segments) down to a ~1.1km radius around Hudson Yards (151 real segments: 7th-11th Ave, Dyer/Galvin Ave, W 30th/34th/40th/41st/42nd St) to keep the embedded payload reasonable for a site-scoped tool. Embedded the same way buildings/boroughs already are (a <script type="application/json"> tag, not a live fetch, keeping the file:// + offline-friendly convention this whole app follows). Added a "Major streets" toggle in the Context panel section.

v3.0.25 - 2026-08-13 - Follow-up to v3.0.24: Joe zoomed into the Manhattan-context view and found the red "you are here" site-boundary outline visually competing with/cutting across the now-visible real buildings, asked for it gone. Removed the outline entirely rather than repositioning it -- with the real buildings staying visible in this view since v3.0.24, they ARE the "you are here" marker now; a separate outline drawn on top of them is redundant clutter, not a missing feature.

v3.0.24 - 2026-08-13 - Joe pointed out that hiding the real buildings and ground plane while zoomed out to Manhattan-context scale (done in v3.0.20-23 to avoid a then-opaque borough platform burying the tiny site models) defeated the entire point of a context view -- "we need to see the building forms in relation to context." Reworked the Manhattan layer from a tall (400m) opaque platform into a thin ground PLATE instead: extruded DOWNWARD so its top surface sits at z=0, exactly where the real buildings' own bases already are (same role the small square tan ground plane already plays at site scale, just Manhattan-shaped and much larger). With the platform no longer tall enough to bury anything, buildings and the negative-space mesh stay fully visible throughout Manhattan-context mode, now visibly rising up out of the real island shape in their correct real-world position. Still hides only the now-redundant square tan ground plane (Manhattan's real shape replaces its role) and the cutaway-editing-specific extras (drag handles, section cut-lines, poché cap fills) -- those are about a different close-up editing workflow, unrelated to city-scale context viewing.

v3.0.23 - 2026-08-13 - Joe screenshotted the ground plane getting diagonally sliced off while just zooming out normally in the DEFAULT (non-Manhattan-context) view. Root cause: OrbitControls had no maxDistance set, so an ordinary scroll-to-zoom-out could push the camera further from the target than camera.far (4000), clipping the ground plane's far edge against the far clip plane. Fixed with controls.maxDistance capped just under camera.far, synced with the same ORIGINAL/BOROUGH-mode pattern already used for near/far -- had to be set BEFORE controls.update() in both zoomToBoroughContext() and exitBoroughContext(), since OrbitControls clamps the camera's position to maxDistance on every update() call regardless of how that position was set, which broke the borough-context camera positioning too until the ordering was fixed.

v3.0.22 - 2026-08-13 - Joe asked whether to add a toggle for Queens/Brooklyn in the Manhattan-context view or just drop them -- agreed with dropping them: Hudson Yards isn't geographically near either borough, and they were adding visual clutter (and real camera-framing complexity) for a "you are here" marker that only ever needed Manhattan's own outline to make its point. Simplified the context layer to Manhattan-only; the Queens/Brooklyn data stays embedded in the page but unused, cheap to bring back later if wanted. Tightened BOROUGH_CAMERA_FAR from 60000 to 20000 to match the now-smaller Manhattan-only extent, improving depth-buffer precision further as a side benefit.

v3.0.21 - 2026-08-13 - Joe reported "extrusions are very high" in the new Manhattan-context view, plus zoom/pan/culling glitchiness. Root cause 1: the borough-context camera reused the SITE's own low ~25 degree grazing elevation angle -- the right cinematic angle for a 1.2km site with tall buildings, but at ~30km borough scale that same low angle means looking at the extruded borough platform almost edge-on, making even a modest extrusion height look like a sheer cliff. Root cause 2: near=1/far=60000 is a 60000:1 depth-buffer ratio, far beyond what an 8-bit-ish depth buffer can meaningfully resolve, which is what showed up as the zoom/pan/culling glitches. Fixed with a dedicated, much steeper (68 degree) aerial angle specifically for the borough-context view, and a much larger near plane (30, up from 1) while in that mode; reduced BOROUGH_DEPTH from 700 to 400 since the steeper viewing angle no longer needs a tall platform to read clearly as extruded.

v3.0.20 - 2026-08-13 - Joe pointed at Subway Pulse's own borough rendering and asked why Negative Space's Manhattan-context layer couldn't do the same -- inspected Subway Pulse's actual live bundle directly (not guessing): it builds each borough as a real THREE.ExtrudeGeometry solid (MeshStandardMaterial, no bevel, real lit shading), not a flat 2D fill. Ported the same technique here, but kept this project's own established ink/muted grayscale palette rather than Subway Pulse's fully-saturated per-borough colors, to preserve Negative Space's own visual identity (grayscale mass-vs-void plus one reserved accent color, not a multi-hue map). This also addressed a real structural risk Joe suspected independently: the old flat borough fills sat mere centimeters off the ground plane at real city (~30km) scale -- exactly the kind of near-coincident geometry that caused this project's earlier z-fighting bugs (v3.0.7's dithering, v3.0.10's stencil degeneracy). Had to hide the real buildings/ground while zoomed to city scale in this version, since the new ~700m-thick opaque extruded platform would otherwise completely bury the tiny site models sitting inside its footprint (this tradeoff was corrected in v3.0.24 once the platform was reworked to be thin instead of tall).

v3.0.19 - 2026-08-13 - Joe pointed at SPIRA's GitHub repo and asked why its colors weren't fully in use, since the app's dark theme didn't look right -- turned out v3.0.12 only ever captured SPIRA's LIGHT theme (getComputedStyle on the live site only sees whichever theme happens to be active at read time). The dark palette shipped in Negative Space up to this point was invented from scratch and didn't resemble SPIRA's real dark theme at all, which SPIRA's own CSS explicitly calls "Blueprint" -- pale blue ink on deep blue paper, a genuinely different design, not just an inverted version of the light theme. Pulled the real dark-theme values directly from SPIRA's GitHub source (not the live site, to get the actual authored values with certainty) and replaced Negative Space's invented dark palette with them.

v3.0.18 - 2026-08-13 - Same session as v3.0.17's compass fix, same underlying category of bug: Joe reported clicking the S nav button made him look north instead of south. Root cause: setOrthogonalView() was built on an architectural "elevation" convention -- the label names which SIDE you stand on and look FROM (like a "North Elevation" drawing, camera positioned north of the model looking south AT its north face), not "press N, look toward north." Fixed by placing the camera on the OPPOSITE side from the button's label and facing toward it instead, matching the more intuitive "press a compass direction, face that direction" convention most people expect from a labeled nav button.

v3.0.17 - 2026-08-13 - Joe reported "true north is incorrect" on the new nav compass. Root cause: updateCompass() computed the needle's rotation from the offset FROM the target TO the camera (camera.position minus controls.target) instead of the camera's actual forward/facing vector (target minus camera.position) -- those two vectors point in exactly opposite directions. Confirmed by hand: clicking N correctly moved the camera to face true north, but with the old formula the needle pointed at the fixed "N" label on the dial as if the camera were facing that way from BEHIND itself. Fixed by flipping the subtraction order.

v3.0.16 - 2026-08-13 - Joe reported the new Manhattan-context borough outline looked broken/invisible. Root cause: the outline's color was hardcoded to the light-theme --ink value (near-black), which is almost the identical color to dark theme's near-black background -- invisible not because of a positioning or rendering bug, but because Joe's session happened to be in dark theme (persisted from an earlier session's own theme toggle) when the color was chosen without checking. Fixed by reading colors live via getComputedStyle instead of hardcoding a single theme's values, re-synced on every theme toggle so it stays correct if the user switches themes later without reloading.

v3.0.15 - 2026-08-13 - Built the Manhattan-context feature, the first of the two remaining backlog items (Manhattan island context + major streets). Embedded real NYC borough outlines (Manhattan/Queens/Brooklyn, from a public click_that_hood redistribution of NYC Open Data's Borough Boundaries dataset, simplified via shapely to roughly 350-550 points per borough to keep the embedded payload reasonable) as flat ShapeGeometry fills + LineLoop outlines, projected through the exact same project(lon,lat) origin the real buildings already use so everything shares one consistent coordinate frame. Added a "you are here" site-footprint marker, a Manhattan-context toggle button, and zoomToBoroughContext()/exitBoroughContext() camera framing to transition between the close-up site view and the wider city-context view. This feature went through many follow-up rounds (v3.0.16 through v3.0.25) as Joe caught real issues testing each one live.
v3.0.14 - 2026-08-13 - Fixed a real showstopper Joe caught: the app loaded to a completely blank viewport, no error visible, stats panel stuck on its "-" placeholders. Reconnected live via Claude-in-Chrome and read the console directly instead of guessing -- found an uncaught TypeError at the exact line where v3.0.11's drag-handle code calls scene.add(heightHandle, xHandle, yHandle). That call sits at the SCRIPT'S TOP LEVEL (runs synchronously on initial parse), but scene is declared as `let scene` and stays undefined until initScene() runs -- which only happens later, inside the async boot() IIFE further down the same file. The uncaught top-level error killed the entire module script at that point: initScene(), initManifold(), and loadData() -- everything boot() was supposed to call -- never ran at all. That's why nothing rendered and nothing errored to a visible toast (the toast calls themselves never executed). Moved the scene.add() call out of top-level scope into boot(), right after initScene() so scene actually exists by then. Fixing that surfaced a SECOND, identical latent bug one step further down -- the drag-handle pointerdown/pointermove listeners were registered via renderer.domElement.addEventListener(...), also at top level, also before renderer exists. Wrapped both listener registrations in a new attachHandleDragListeners() function, called from boot() right after initScene() alongside the scene.add() fix. Scanned the rest of the top-level script (everything between the handle code and boot()) for the same pattern (scene./renderer./camera./controls. accessed outside a function before boot() runs) -- found no further instances. Live-verified the complete fix on Joe's actual localhost session before shipping (not just reasoned about it): reloaded, console clean (no exceptions), window.__NS.scene populated with 7 children, statBuildings/statParts showing real numbers (497 / 254+243 fallback) instead of placeholders, and a screenshot confirming the real Hudson Yards massing renders correctly. Root-cause note for future sessions: any code added at the true top level of this module script (outside a function body) that touches scene/renderer/camera/controls will hit this same failure mode, since those aren't assigned until initScene() runs inside boot() -- new top-level feature code should either live inside a function called from boot() after initScene(), or be added inside initScene()/boot() directly, never as bare top-level statements.

v3.0.13 - 2026-08-12 - Two of Joe's 4-item backlog request, the two self-contained ones (the other two -- Manhattan context from Subway Pulse, major streets from PLUTO Massing -- need real data-source/projection work, queued next). (1) Basic 3D nav: N/S/E/W orthogonal-view buttons, Recenter, and a rotating north compass -- same pattern Joe's PLUTO Massing tool already established (real 3D orbit/pan/zoom + recenter + north compass + plan view), ported to this scene's own Z-up/OrbitControls rig rather than copied wholesale since the camera scale and default framing differ. Direction buttons position the camera along the corresponding world axis at a slight vertical lift (pure edge-on reads as a flat line) preserving the current camera-target distance, so the zoom level someone is already at carries over. Recenter fits to the real computed site bounds once a compute has run, falling back to the scene's original default view before that. The compass needle rotates opposite the camera's azimuth around the target (atan2(dx,dy) against the world +Y = true-north convention every sibling tool already uses), same behavior as a real handheld compass or a map app's rotation indicator. Live-verified the direction math on Joe's actual session before shipping: N/E/S/W azimuths came back exactly 0/90/180/-90 degrees, and a real DOM click on the injected N button produced the identical view as the isolated camera-math test. (2) Metric/Imperial units toggle, same convention as Joe's own SPIRA tool (Metric (m)/Imperial (ft)) -- display-only: a new formatLength() helper is the only thing that changed, called everywhere a cut label was previously hardcoded to Xm; every slider value, clip-plane constant, and the geometry itself stay in real meters throughout, exactly as before.

v3.0.12 - 2026-08-12 - Joe said he liked the color scheme from his own SPIRA tool (spiraformdeform.netlify.app) and asked to bring it over. Extracted SPIRA's actual light-theme values via getComputedStyle on the live site rather than guessing from memory: body background #F4F1EA, panel background rgba(251,250,246,0.92), body text #1A1A18, muted/label text #7A7568, and confirmed its --brick CSS custom property is exactly #B23A2E -- the same brick-red documented as the baseline Corbusier-polychromy accent across Joe's whole tool suite (generative-design-tools.md), not a new color. Updated the root CSS variables (--paper, --card, --ink, --ink-soft, --line, --accent, --accent-deep) to these values, plus the theme-color meta tag and both scene.background references that were still hardcoded to the old #F1E7D2. Also shifted the exterior mass color a second time -- v3.0.11 moved it from the original terracotta red to a warm light gray, but SPIRA's own deformed solid reads as a cool steel-blue-gray against the same cream chrome, so matched that instead (0x8798A6, with the onBeforeCompile front-facing vec3 updated to match). Attempted direct pixel-sampling of SPIRA's live WebGL canvas (readPixels, then a 2D-canvas drawImage fallback) to get an exact solid color instead of eyeballing it, but both approaches consistently returned the page background -- the canvas almost certainly uses preserveDrawingBuffer:false, so its buffer is cleared before an external script can read it after the fact; noting this so a future session doesn't re-attempt the same dead end. Fell back to a careful visual read of the live screenshot instead. Live-tested the full palette (UI chrome + 3D material) directly on Joe's running session, including a fresh Compute (to confirm the new negMat color survives a real mesh rebuild, not just a one-off material swap) -- reads cleanly cohesive with the cream background and brick-red accent buttons.

v3.0.11 - 2026-08-12 - Two of Joe's requests: (1) the exterior surface's terracotta red felt heavy -- changed to a light, airy warm gray (0xD8D3C6, matching the vec3 in negMat's onBeforeCompile front-facing branch too) that still shares the same warm undertone as the site's cream/tan palette. (2) draggable 3D handles on the cutaway planes so the section can be pushed/pulled directly in the viewport instead of only via the 2D sliders. Added three small steel-blue cone handles (one per axis: height, X, Y), positioned at the corner of the exposed cross-section -- the one corner that is always part of the real exposed cut edge regardless of where the sliders currently sit, since the octant carve always removes material toward siteMinX/siteMinY/0. Dragging uses the standard single-axis gizmo technique: raycast against an invisible plane containing the drag axis and facing the camera, take the intersection point's coordinate along that axis as the new cut value, clamp to the same dead-zone-padded slider range v3.0.10 established, and feed it through new setHeightCut/setXCutaway/setYCutaway functions -- refactored out of the slider input handlers so both input paths (slider drag, viewport handle drag) run the exact same update code, never two copies to keep in sync. OrbitControls is disabled for the duration of a handle drag so the two gestures do not fight over the same mouse. Verified the core ray-plane math live in an isolated test (clean camera state, no OrbitControls interference): dragging produced smooth, well-behaved axis-only movement (about 1.7 world units per screen pixel at that zoom, the other two axes staying essentially pinned). Note on testing: an earlier live-test attempt referenced a nonexistent ns.controls debug-hook property while trying to disable OrbitControls mid-test, which silently failed and let the synthetic drag events orbit the live camera instead -- purely a test-harness bug, not present in the shipped code, which correctly uses the real module-scoped controls variable (same one animate() already calls .update() on); flagged here so a future debugging session does not waste time on the same false lead.

v3.0.10 - 2026-08-12 - Joe reported a large noisy/streaked phantom panel replacing the correct black cavity wall or floor when the cutaway sliders sat near their extremes -- his own screenshots showed it on the X cutaway near -577/-578m and separately on the height cut at exactly 0m, and he'd found that nudging a plane by ~1mm made it go away. Reconnected live and reproduced both exactly: the X cutaway slider's own minimum was set to the site's raw geometric boundary (minX/minY) with no margin -- unlike the max side, which already had a DEAD_ZONE_MARGIN from the v3.0.4-era roof/dead-zone fix -- so dragging to that extreme puts the cutaway plane exactly coincident with the model's own outer wall. The height slider's min (0) hits the same degeneracy against the cavity's own real floor, but can't be range-padded the same way since 0 is a real, intentionally-reachable value (see the v3.0.6 changelog on why the floor was left reachable). Root mechanism in both cases: the poché fill's stencil-capping technique (createPlaneStencilGroup) counts front/back face crossings of the plane to determine "inside solid" -- when the plane sits EXACTLY on real geometry, that count degenerates, and the fill quad's stencil test falls back to painting almost the entire oversized quad instead of just the true cross-section, producing the large streaky panel. Fixed both sides: (1) padded the X/Y sliders' min by the same DEAD_ZONE_MARGIN already used on max, and (2) for the case that can't be range-padded (height=0, and defense-in-depth for X/Y too), added separate epsilon-nudged (1cm) plane copies used ONLY inside the stencil parity pass -- never for the quad's own position or the real visible clipping, which stay exactly at the user's slider value. Live-verified both fixes directly against Joe's exact reported slider values before shipping: the X-boundary panel and the height=0 floor streaking are both gone, replaced by the correct black surface with a clean red edge trace.

v3.0.9 - 2026-08-12 - Reconnected live to Joe's localhost (port 8888) via Claude-in-Chrome and finally tested v3.0.8's flatShading fix directly instead of shipping another guess. It did not work, and testing live also overturned the whole diagnosis: (1) Read three.js's actual FLAT_SHADED fragment shader source -- it computes its lighting normal as cross(dFdx(vViewPosition), dFdy(vViewPosition)), straight off the real interpolated triangle position, and completely ignores any normal attribute on the geometry. So flatShading could never have used a cleaned-up normal even if we supplied one -- it always re-derives the raw, noisy per-triangle geometry as the shading normal. (2) Built and Node-verified a real fix for that specific mechanism anyway: unioned triangles across their real shared edges (5cm vertex matching) into coplanar patches wherever the angle between them was under 2deg, and assigned each patch one averaged normal as an explicit attribute, with flatShading turned off so the shader actually uses it. Verified against the real 497-building mesh: of 10,875 genuine same-face edge-adjacent triangle pairs, 100% became exactly identical after clustering; of 9,009 real-corner pairs, 0 were wrongly merged; mesh positions (Z: 0..395m, 13,806 triangles) confirmed untouched. This is a real, shipped improvement (see buildCoplanarPatchNormals) -- but live-testing it directly on Joe's session (patching the running scene via window.__NS before touching the file) showed it does NOT fix the visible hatching Joe's been seeing. (3) Diagnosed further live: swapped negativeMesh to a fully UNLIT MeshBasicMaterial (no lighting/normal computation possible at all) and the hatching was still clearly visible -- conclusively ruling out every material/shading/normal theory from v3.0.7 through this version. Ray-cast through the hatched screen pixels looking for near-duplicate/z-fighting surfaces (the v2.4.0-era bug's signature) -- found none; hit distances were hundreds of meters apart, not near-coincident. Then tested supersampling: bumped renderer.setPixelRatio from 1.5 to 4 and the pattern visibly, substantially thinned out. That result points to the real mechanism: this is rasterization aliasing of genuinely fine real geometric detail (many thin setback walls and small building parts, viewed at a distance and at grazing angles from inside the excavated void) -- a rendering-resolution limitation, not a bug in the mesh, materials, or normals. No further material/geometry patch will fix it; the lever is rendering quality (higher supersampling, or geometric LOD/simplification at distance), which has real performance cost and needs Joe's call rather than being silently cranked up. Shipped the coplanar-patch normal fix (a real, if secondary, improvement) and this corrected diagnosis. Also added a "High detail (slower)" checkbox that bumps the renderer's pixel ratio 2x above the base device ratio (e.g. Joe's session: 1.5 -> 3) -- opt-in rather than always-on since it's a real frame-time cost on a 13,800+ triangle scene. Live-tested at 3x directly on Joe's running session (not just the isolated 4x test above) and confirmed a clear, meaningful reduction in the mid-distance mottling. The underlying fine geometric detail is still there at full resolution -- this doesn't eliminate every trace of it, just makes it substantially less visually noisy -- so the primary hatching is considered mitigated (opt-in) rather than fully resolved.

v3.0.8 - 2026-08-12 - Joe's screenshot showed the hatched noise still present after v3.0.7's GL_DITHER fix. Reconsidered the mechanism: the pattern in his close-up screenshot is much denser/more irregular than the clean 2-color alternation the dither fix was verified against (that test was on a trivial single-normal flat plane, where dithering really is the only possible explanation). The cavity walls are part of a much more complex, connected mesh, and computeVertexNormals() smooths normals across shared vertices -- including wherever a nominally-flat wall shares a vertex with a genuinely different-angle face (a floor, another wall). That leaks slightly-off normals onto triangles that should be one uniform flat color, showing up as fine per-triangle shading noise at grazing angles -- a real per-vertex normal data issue, not something GL_DITHER touches at all. Added flatShading:true to negMat and the Buildings-layer material, which tells the shader to compute each triangle's normal directly from its own geometry instead of the smoothed per-vertex data, so every triangle renders as one genuinely uniform flat color with nothing left to look noisy. NOT verified visually -- the browser/localhost connection was unreachable for this entire round, same as v3.0.6/v3.0.7's later portion. Also scoped (not yet built) Joe's separate request for the red cut-line to trace the poché fill's own boundary -- the poché quad is a separate GPU-stencil-clipped surface, not part of the mesh the edge outlines are drawn from, so it currently has no line of its own. Real fix needs either a proper mesh-plane cross-section extraction or a stencil-based edge-detection pass -- meaningfully bigger than today's fixes, deliberately not rushed without the ability to test it live.

v3.0.7 - 2026-08-12 - Attempted a direct fix for the hatched/speckled noise Joe marked with X's on the ground plane and walls in a v3.0.6 screenshot. This is the same artifact diagnosed a few versions back (see the v3.0.3-era investigation): direct pixel sampling via gl.readPixels found the ground plane's two dominant colors were (153,142,109) and (154,142,110), alternating pixel to pixel -- a 1-level channel difference, which is exactly the signature of WebGL's GL_DITHER capability (on by default) spreading a smooth gradient across neighboring pixels' 8-bit values to fake tones the framebuffer can't represent exactly. Disabled it directly on the WebGL context (renderer.getContext().disable(gl.DITHER)) rather than fighting it indirectly through materials or lighting changes -- this targets the exact confirmed mechanism, not a new guess. One tradeoff worth knowing: disabling dithering means any genuine underlying gradient will now round to the nearest flat 8-bit step instead of being smoothed, which COULD show as visible banding instead of noise if the gradient turns out to be larger than expected -- most likely outcome for a subtle gradient is it just renders as a single flat color with no artifact at all, but flagging the possibility since this could not be verified visually this round. The browser connection was unreachable for this entire round (both Joe's localhost server and, separately, the Claude-in-Chrome connection itself) -- shipped on the strength of the earlier pixel-level diagnosis alone. Needs Joe's eyes.

v3.0.6 - 2026-08-12 - Replaced the filled section-cut highlight with a real line-drawing outline, per Joe's request to get rid of the red fill. Root cause of the fill: wherever the current cut plane coincides with a large PRE-EXISTING flat surface -- not just the model's own roof at capHeight (v3.0.4's dead-zone case) but also, as Joe's screenshot showed, the floor of the excavated cavity itself, which is a genuinely large flat surface at z=0 by design -- the thin clipped band has no way to distinguish "a real thin cut trace" from "a coincidentally-aligned whole flat face," so it fills the entire coincident surface. There's no slider value to pad away from at the floor the way v3.0.4 padded the roof, since cutting right down to z=0 is a real, intended thing to do here. Real fix: stopped filling the clipped region entirely. Now extracts the actual geometric edges of the final mesh once via THREE.EdgesGeometry (a hard-edge/silhouette extraction, not every internal triangulation seam -- verified in a Node harness against the real watertight Manifold mesh: ~9,300 edges at a 15deg threshold, full z=0..395 coverage, builds in under a second) and draws those, clipped to the same thin band + v3.0.5's corrected negated-plane restriction. Switched the line material's depthTest off rather than reuse v3.0.3's polygon-offset fix, since WebGL polygon offset only applies to polygon/fill rasterization and silently does nothing for line primitives -- reintroducing that exact depth-fighting bug unverified would have been worse than just disabling depth test outright, which is also standard practice for architectural section-cut lines (an always-visible annotation overlay, not something meant to compete for Z-buffer priority). NOT visually verified this round -- the browser connection itself became unresponsive mid-session (separate from the earlier localhost-not-running issue), so this shipped on Node-verified geometry alone. Needs Joe's eyes before it's confirmed.

v3.0.5 - 2026-08-12 - Found the actual bug behind the "full volume cut lines, can't see x/y sectioning" report -- the v3.0.4 slider-margin fix was addressing a real but secondary issue; this is the primary one. The cut-line highlight (buildCutLineMeshes) was restricting each axis's thin trace band to the OTHER two axes' RAW (unnegated) clip planes -- the exact same mistake the poché cap fill had before it was fixed in v2.7.0. Since negMat removes the octant only where ALL three conditions are exceeded together (clipIntersection=true), restricting the highlight to the other axes' normal "kept" side traces real geometry across the entire kept region (nearly the whole site, once a slider sits near its own max), not specifically the boundary of the actual removed corner. This bug has been there since v2.5.0 but was invisible until v3.0.3's depth-test fix -- before that, nothing in the highlight rendered at all, so the wrong restriction never had a chance to show. Fixed the same way the cap fill was: swapped in the already-existing negated planes (xClipPlaneNeg/yClipPlaneNeg/sectionPlaneNeg, sitting unused right there since v2.7.0). Verified live against the real running scene (not guessed): at Joe's exact reported slider state, the old restriction matched 45 triangles scattered across most of the site; the fixed restriction matches only 4, and those are exactly the two exterior wall faces meeting at the real removed corner -- nothing else.

v3.0.4 - 2026-08-12 - Fixed the "can't see x/y sectioning when height is maxed" confusion Joe flagged with screenshots. Root cause: each of the 3 cutaway sliders has a "dead" extreme where the octant-removal condition becomes mathematically impossible to satisfy -- for HEIGHT CUT that's exactly capHeight (395m in this dataset), since nothing in the model is EVER taller than the model's own cap height, so "remove where height > slider value" removes nothing at all once the slider reaches that value, regardless of where X/Y are set (same dead zone exists at maxX for the X slider and maxY for the Y slider, just less likely to be dragged there by accident). The section-cut highlight made this more confusing rather than less: at that exact height it happens to coincide with the model's own already-flat roof, so it lights up the entire top face and looks exactly like a big cut just happened, when none did. Fix: pulled each slider's usable max back by a small margin (3x the highlight band's own half-thickness) so the dead zone is never reachable from the UI -- there's always at least a sliver of real cutaway, and the highlight always traces a real thin boundary instead of coincidentally overlapping a whole flat face.

v3.0.3 - 2026-08-12 - Found and fixed the actual "no cutaway line displaying" bug live via the browser connection, and separately got hard confirmation on the ground-plane hatching -- both by directly reading real WebGL/pixel data through window.__NS instead of guessing.

Cut-line fix: confirmed via a live bisection (successively testing depthTest:false, then polygon-offset magnitudes from -4 up to -100000, reading real gl.readPixels output after each change) that the red cut-line polygons were consistently LOSING the depth test against the coincident negativeMesh surface directly beneath them. -4/-4 polygon offset is a typical safe magnitude for a normal-scale scene, but this site spans 1000+ meters with the camera ~800m out, where standard (non-reversed) depth-buffer precision is coarse enough that -4 does essentially nothing. Measured -5000 as the point where visible coverage fully matched the depthTest:false ceiling (15,015 red pixels either way), stable with no visible peeling up to -100000; shipped -6000 for margin. (Camera near/far adjustment was tested as an alternative fix and empirically ruled out -- redCount barely moved across near=1/50/100 at a fixed offset, so the offset magnitude was the actual lever, not depth-buffer range.) Confirmed visually afterward: a clean red outline correctly stepping in and out around the building silhouettes at the cut height, exactly the feature v2.5.0 originally intended.

Ground-plane hatching: sampled raw canvas pixels (gl.readPixels) across the ground plane and found the two dominant colors there are (153,142,109) and (154,142,110) -- a 1-level difference in the R and B channels, alternating pixel-to-pixel. That is the signature of dithering: the renderer alternating between the two nearest representable 8-bit color values to approximate a smooth lighting gradient that 8-bit channels can't represent exactly. Confirmed on the ground plane specifically because it rules out every geometry-based theory at once -- it's a flat, 2-triangle, non-CSG, non-Manifold mesh, so there is nothing in this file's mesh pipeline left to blame. This is a rendering/color-precision effect (this GPU/browser/driver's dithering behavior), not a bug in the negative-space geometry, and isn't something further changes to this file's meshes could fix.

v3.0.2 - 2026-08-12 - Investigated Joe's "no cutaway line displaying" report live via the Claude-in-Chrome browser connection instead of guessing from screenshots -- first real payoff of that connection this session. Confirmed a genuine, fully reproducible bug: a canvas-wide pixel scan (via gl.readPixels) found ZERO red pixels anywhere on screen with "Section fill (poché)" checked, and toggling that checkbox OFF with nothing else changed made the red cut lines reappear clearly across the whole scene -- not a thin-line visibility problem (already fixed in v3.0.1), a real interaction bug between the poché stencil rendering and the cut-line rendering that comes after it. First theory (stencil test left enabled, discarding the cut-line draws) turned out to be wrong on inspection of the actual three.js source shipped in this app (WebGLState.js): stencil test is unconditionally reset to match each material's own stencilWrite flag before every single draw call, with no caching path that could leave it stuck -- so a same-material "force it off" workaround would have been a no-op dressed up as a fix, and shipping it with confident language would have been exactly the kind of unverified claim this project has been trying to avoid. Removed that attempt rather than ship it. Added a small debug hook instead (window.__NS, exposing scene/renderer/camera/negativeMesh/cut-line meshes/clip planes as live getters) -- module-scoped variables aren't reachable from outside an ES module, which is what's been forcing screenshot-and-theorize debugging all session; this unlocks actually inspecting the live WebGL/three.js state through the browser connection instead. Needs Joe to reload with this version before the real mechanism can be pinned down.

v3.0.1 - 2026-08-12 - Two fixes from Joe's v3.0.0 test screenshots. (1) The section-cut red line (v2.5.0) already exists but wasn't reading as visible: thickened from 0.4m to 2.0m half-thickness (site scale is 1000m+, so 0.8m total was effectively sub-pixel at a normal zoomed-out view) and changed its color from 0xFF3B2E to pure 0xFF0000 -- the old color was too close in hue to the poché cap fill's terracotta (0xB23A2E, near-identical G/B channels) to read clearly against it. (2) Joe reported the hatched texture is ALSO present on the ground plane -- a plain 2-triangle THREE.PlaneGeometry with a flat color, completely unrelated to the CSG pipeline or the v3.0.0 Manifold swap. This is a significant new data point: something that was never geometrically broken shows the same artifact, which rules out mesh/CSG correctness as the cause entirely. Leading hypothesis now is a rendering-level effect -- most likely Windows/Chrome's color dithering on smoothly-lit flat surfaces (a real driver/OS behavior that adds fine noise to fight 8-bit banding), not fixable by anything in this file. Asked Joe for a light-mode comparison screenshot to test this before chasing it further as a geometry problem.

v3.0.0 - 2026-08-12 - Swapped the CSG engine from three-bvh-csg to Manifold (manifold-3d, a WASM library whose entire design goal is *guaranteed*-manifold boolean output). This followed Joe finding a new "two competing masses" gap artifact in the Y-axis cap fill on top of v2.8.0's leftover hatching -- rather than another tolerance patch, dug into the actual root cause with a Node harness: raw building extrusions are perfectly watertight (0% non-manifold edges, tested on 40 real buildings), but after going through three-bvh-csg's ADDITION/SUBTRACTION, ~49% of edges became non-manifold -- identical whether the site was grid-chunked or subtracted as one piece (proven with an apples-to-apples test on the same 15-building subset both ways). The CSG library itself was producing broken geometry; every hatching/gap fix this session had been patching symptoms downstream of that, not the cause. Verified Manifold actually solves it before committing to the rewrite: same 15-building subset went from 49% hole edges to 0%, and the full 497-building site computed in 0.7s (vs ~240s) with 0% hole edges, confirmed independently (not just trusting Manifold's own claim) via a from-scratch edge-sharing check. This eliminated the need for the entire 90m grid-chunking scheme (it existed only to dodge three-bvh-csg's compounding-slowdown pathology, which Manifold doesn't have) and the whole mergeVertices/stripDegenerateTriangles/removeDuplicateCoplanarPairs cleanup chain (they existed only to patch a broken boolean op; Manifold's output needs no cleanup). Compute is now synchronous-fast enough that the async yield-per-building/per-cell scaffolding is gone too. Loaded from unpkg exactly like three.js/OrbitControls already are (confirmed manifold-3d is published there); its internal wasm fetch resolves against that same https:// URL, so it's unaffected by the page itself being opened via file:// -- the file:// fetch restriction that broke things in v1.0.1 only blocks fetching OTHER local files from a file:// page, not a page's own CDN-hosted script/wasm dependencies. Verified against the ACTUAL shipped outerRingPolygon/project functions (extracted from the real file, not reimplemented) run through the real embedded Hudson Yards data end-to-end: 0.8s, 13,806 triangles (down from 44,404 -- no more duplicate seam geometry to begin with), 0% hole edges. Not yet confirmed in-browser -- the wasm-loading-from-unpkg path and the actual visual result (is the hatching/gap artifact really gone) both still need Joe's machine.

v2.8.0 - 2026-08-12 - Fixed the hatched/speckled grazing-angle wall artifact Joe flagged in v2.7.0 screenshots -- confirmed via a toggle test (poché checkbox on/off at the same cut position, hatching identical either way) that this was baked into the negativeMesh geometry itself, unrelated to the new cap-fill code, and a separate resurgence of the same-looking artifact from v1.4.0/v1.6.0. Diagnosed with a Node harness against the real embedded Hudson Yards data rather than guessing: first ruled out the site's actual exterior perimeter walls as the source (measured clean -- exact expected area, zero duplicate/sliver triangles).
