Then found the real cause by widening the v2.6.0 dedup pass's search tolerance in stages: near-duplicate coplanar triangle pairs climbed from 0 (confirming v2.6.0 does its own narrow job perfectly) to 8% of all triangles at a 15cm tolerance -- a much bigger population than the 25-cell sample that set v2.6.0's tight 2-3cm tolerance had suggested. Checking those wider matches' face directions found the real bug in v2.6.0's own logic: 3129 of 3139 matched pairs were SAME-facing (literal redundant copies of one real surface, left behind by three-bvh-csg re-triangulating the same touching cell boundary more than once during the 130-cell ADDITION merge), not the opposite-facing degenerate membranes the pass was designed around -- meaning v2.6.0's "drop both members of every matched pair" was correct for the rare opposite-facing case but wrong for the common same-facing case, which is textbook z-fighting (two near-identical, barely-offset copies of the same surface, invisible head-on, flickering per-pixel at grazing angles). Fix: widened the tolerance (centroidTol/vertTol 0.02m/0.03m -> 0.15m) and branched on face direction -- opposite-facing pairs still drop both (as before), same-facing pairs now keep exactly one copy and drop only the redundant duplicate. Verified safe end-to-end with the actual shipped function (extracted from the real file, not reimplemented) run through the full real pipeline: bounding box byte-identical before/after (-577.70..577.70 x -412.96..412.96 x 0..395), final mesh 42,849 triangles (down from 44,404 -- the 1,555 genuine redundant/degenerate triangles removed). Not yet re-confirmed visually on Joe's machine -- the mechanism (real z-fighting from real duplicate geometry) is now solidly evidenced, but whether it fully resolves the visible hatching still needs his eyes.

v2.7.0 - 2026-08-12 - Added the dark cut fill (architectural section poché) Joe asked for on a screenshot: a solid dark fill now appears wherever a cutaway plane actually slices through solid material, instead of the cavity interior reading as hanging in space with no floor at the cut. Clipping planes only discard fragments -- there's no cap surface generated at a cut on their own -- so this uses the standard three.js stencil-capping technique (from three.js's own webgl_clipping_stencil example): for each of the 3 cutaway axes, the final mesh is rendered twice into the stencil buffer only (front/back faces, INCREMENT/DECREMENT, clipped to that one axis plane) so stencil ends up non-zero exactly where the solid crosses the plane, then a large flat quad at that plane is drawn with a NotEqual(0) stencil test so it only paints where solid was actually cut. Our cutaway removes only one octant (clipIntersection=true -- removed where all 3 axis conditions are exceeded together), unlike the three.js demo's independent half-space slabs, so each cap quad had to be restricted to the OTHER two planes' NEGATED (exceeded) halves rather than their normal kept halves -- otherwise the fill would show even where nothing was actually carved. Negated plane copies track the live sliders' constants via a new syncCapFillPlanes(), called alongside the existing updateCutLinePlanes() at all 4 places a slider or Reset cutaway changes a plane. Added a "Section fill (poché)" checkbox (default on) so it can be compared against the un-filled view. Verified the JS syntax and the plane-restriction logic by hand against the reference three.js implementation; the stencil buffer itself is a real GPU feature this sandbox has no WebGL/GPU context to render and check (same limitation noted for the v1.4.0/v1.6.0 render artifacts) -- needs Joe's confirmation on his own machine before this is considered done.

v2.6.0 - 2026-08-11 - Fixed the residual hatching artifact from v2.4.0's seam fix, which Joe confirmed helped a lot but didn't fully eliminate it (marked the remaining bad areas directly on a screenshot). Measured why in a Node harness: even after properly CSG-ADDITION-merging the 130 grid cells, ~868 pairs of near-duplicate coplanar triangles remained in a 25-cell test region -- only 61 of those were within mergeVertices' 1mm weld tolerance; the rest were 1-20mm apart, small enough in world space to z-fight visually (especially at a distance or grazing angle, exactly where Joe's screenshots showed it) but too far apart for vertex welding to safely merge without risking warping legitimate fine geometry elsewhere. These are residual internal-seam artifacts from the CSG library's own numerical precision when independently re-triangulating touching boundaries during the ADDITION merges. Fix: a new cleanup pass finds pairs of triangles whose all 3 vertices closely correspond to each other (not just a nearby centroid -- that alone would also match two legitimately different, merely-adjacent triangles on the same continuous flat face) and removes both members of each confirmed pair. Verified safe and effective: after removal, the mesh's overall bounding box is unchanged (confirms only internal seam artifacts were touched, not the actual silhouette), and near-duplicate count dropped from 868 to 71 (92% reduction) in the same test region. This time verified against the ACTUAL shipped functions (extracted and run from the real file, not a reimplementation) end-to-end before shipping, after an earlier version of this same verification approach was left incomplete.

v2.5.0 - 2026-08-11 - Added section-cut lines per Joe's request: a bright red line now traces exactly where each cutaway slider is currently slicing through the solid, stepping in and out to follow the real building forms as it crosses them (standard architectural-section convention). Three.js's clipping planes only discard fragments past the plane -- there's no actual cap geometry generated at the cut, so the boundary had no visual marker of its own. Implementation: for each of the 3 cutaway axes, the same final geometry is rendered again with an unlit red material clipped to a thin ~0.8m slab straddling that axis's current value, ANDed with the other two axes' normal kept-region planes, so only a thin red sliver survives -- exactly tracing the current cut boundary. The main mesh uses clipIntersection=true (removes only where all 3 planes agree, for the dollhouse-corner cutaway); the highlight bands need the opposite combination logic, clipIntersection=false (Three.js's union mode), using two new thin-band planes per axis plus the other two axes' existing plane objects directly. Verified the plane math produces the exact intended band in isolation (a center-100, half-thickness-0.4 band correctly keeps z in [99.6, 100.4] and nothing outside it). Confirmed working by Joe directly in the browser (pointed at the visible red line in a screenshot). Separately: Joe reported the v2.4.0 seam fix helped a lot but didn't fully eliminate the hatching artifact -- some remains on specific walls. Holding off on further guessing at the root cause without being able to see it directly (that's what led to the wrong first attempt); waiting on Joe's local server or a zoomed screenshot to diagnose properly rather than shipping another unverified guess.

v2.4.0 - 2026-08-11 - Fixed a real graphics bug in the v2.3.0 grid-chunked compute, caught by Joe: dragging the cutaway sliders revealed a scratchy/hatched noise pattern wherever the cut exposed the mesh interior, instead of a clean flat surface. Root cause: v2.3.0 stitched the 130 grid cells back together by concatenating their raw triangle data, but each cell was carved independently against its own small box -- so every shared boundary between two adjacent cells existed TWICE in the output (once as cell A's wall, once as cell B's matching wall, occupying the exact same plane). Invisible from outside the solid mass, but the moment a cutaway plane exposed the interior, those duplicate overlapping faces fought each other for the same pixels -- exactly the hatched artifact in Joe's screenshots. Fix: instead of concatenating cell geometry, properly CSG-ADDITION-merge all 130 cell Brushes into one continuous solid -- a real boolean union resolves coincident boundaries instead of just gluing triangle soups together. Verified this doesn't reintroduce the freeze problem: ADDITION has never been the pathological operation in any of the SUBTRACTION-focused debugging that led to v2.3.0 (39 adjacent cells merged in 6.36s with no blowup in isolated testing). Verified end-to-end against the real Hudson Yards data with the seam fix included: 99.9s grid compute + 61.0s seam-merge = 161.4s total (up from v2.3.0's 96s, the honest cost of doing this correctly), final mesh is 46,752 triangles (fewer than v2.3.0's 52,168 -- the missing ~5,400 were exactly the duplicate seam faces this fix removes), top face confirmed still reaching the full cap height.

v2.3.0 - 2026-08-11 - Actually fixed the full-site compute freeze, after the v2.2.0 warning-text band-aid was rightly rejected ("that's not a fix, it's just describing the problem"). Root cause found via extensive Node-harness debugging on the real Hudson Yards data, not guessed: three-bvh-csg's SUBTRACTION gets progressively, catastrophically slower as the accumulator geometry gets messier from prior cuts -- confirmed this is about ACCUMULATED complexity in a sequential chain, not building count or complexity alone (an ordinary single-part building measured 143s when it was the 19th cut in a chain, vs 0.04s subtracted alone; separately, one specific 25-part tower is inherently slow, ~38-80s, regardless of chain position, due to its own genuinely complex setback massing). The old "merge all 497 buildings into one solid, then ONE final subtraction against the site box" approach hits this same wall as a single giant blocking call, which is why it never resolved no matter how long you waited. Fix: split the site into a grid of 90m cells (130 cells for this site) so no single subtraction chain ever gets long enough to hit the pathology -- each cell starts fresh from its own small box. Within a cell, each building's own parts are merged together first via ADDITION (always fast, verified) into one clean solid before being subtracted as a single unit, avoiding a second, separate pathology from feeding raw overlapping/adjacent parts straight into sequential SUBTRACTIONs. Yields after every building (not just every cell), bounding the worst possible single blocking chunk to a known, finite outlier (~38s for the one complex tower) instead of an open-ended freeze. Verified end-to-end by running the exact algorithm from the shipped file (not just a standalone test) against the real embedded data: completes in 95.95s total, produces a clean 52,168-triangle final mesh, top face confirmed reaching the full 395m cap height (open mold, not sealed). Actual browser timing not yet confirmed -- Node and browser JS usually perform comparably for this kind of work, but this needs Joe's confirmation before considering it done.

v2.2.0 - 2026-08-11 - Built on the confirmed-working v1.6.0 baseline (a prior chat's attempt at Z-up + async compute regressed the app and was discarded rather than patched further). Two fixes: (1) Mold-height fix, the real bug behind "this reads as a void, not a solid mold" -- the site volume's cap height was tallest-building + 20m padding, so even the tallest tower never reached the top face and the subtraction never punched through anywhere; the top surface stayed fully solid no matter what, which is why the visible cavities read as sealed pits/voids instead of an open mold. Per Joe's exact spec (pour concrete to the height of the tallest building, pull the buildings out): cap height is now exactly the tallest building's height, no padding. Verified in a Node harness against the real Hudson Yards data: result Z now spans 0..395.00m, exactly the tallest building's real height, confirming the top face is genuinely punched through rather than capped by dead air. (2) Z-up conversion (Three.js default is Y-up), matching Revit and Joe's other AEC pipelines -- same conversion as before (extrusion, bounding volume, camera+up vector, orbit target, lights, ground plane, all three clip planes), reverified via the same Node harness: X/Y spans are site-scale (1155x826m) vs Z span building-scale (395m), confirming elevation is on Z. No Web Worker this time -- Workers cannot run at all from a page opened via file:// (confirmed hard Chrome restriction), so the full-site compute will still make the tab look frozen for 1-2 minutes on the final subtraction step; added an explicit warning in the status text and a toast right before that step starts, rather than leaving it looking crashed with no explanation.

v1.6.0 - 2026-08-10 - Addressed live confusion: Joe dragged all three cutaway sliders to their maximum (Height 415m, X 577m, Z 412m), which correctly shows the sealed, uncut exterior of the negative-space solid -- but reads as "nothing happened" since no cavity is exposed at max. Confirmed his GPU renders the quadrant cutaway cleanly with no speckle artifact, so the v1.4.0 concern was indeed the sandbox's software renderer, not a real bug. Added a "Reset cutaway" button that restores all three sliders to the good starting cut (same values auto-applied right after compute) -- factored the slider-init logic that previously only ran once after compute into a reusable resetCutaway() function, called both automatically post-compute and manually via the new button. Reproduced Joe's exact stuck state and the fix in a headless-Chrome run (drag all 3 sliders to max -> confirm sealed-block appearance matches his screenshot -> click Reset cutaway -> confirm it returns to the carved view).

v1.5.0 - 2026-08-10 - Changed Buildings/Negative-space from a mutually-exclusive toggle to two independent on/off switches, per Joe's request -- both layers can now be shown together (buildings visible inside their own carved voids, useful for confirming the carve is accurate) or either one alone. Negative space now auto-enables right after a successful compute instead of requiring a manual switch. Internally: removed the single currentView string state in favor of two independent booleans (showBuildings, showNegative) and a refreshViewToggles() that syncs both buttons' visual state and both layers' .visible flags from them.

v1.4.0 - 2026-08-10 - Added the quadrant cutaway Joe asked for: two more THREE.Plane clip planes (X and Z) alongside the existing height plane, combined with clipIntersection=true so only the single corner octant where all three thresholds are exceeded gets removed -- reveals plan AND two section walls simultaneously, dollhouse-style. Three new sliders (Height/X/Z cutaway). Along the way, fixed the CSG-artifact problem flagged earlier: degenerate triangles cleaned from 31.4% to 0% via mergeVertices(1e-3) + a custom stripDegenerateTriangles pass (caught and fixed a real bug here too -- mergeVertices returns INDEXED geometry, and the naive strip pass assumed the old flat non-indexed layout, silently producing a shattered/garbled mesh until toNonIndexed() was added before stripping, caught via Node harness before shipping). HOWEVER: a speckled red/black noise artifact remains on large flat walls viewed at grazing angles in the quadrant cutaway. Tried two structurally different fixes (separate FrontSide/BackSide meshes, then a single mesh with gl_FrontFacing-based shading) -- both produced the IDENTICAL speckle pattern pixel-for-pixel, which points strongly at the headless-Chrome/SwiftShader software renderer used for automated verification in this sandbox rather than a real code bug. Verified triangle winding on the affected wall is 100% consistent (8/8 correct) via Node harness, ruling out the geometry itself. NOT YET CONFIRMED clean or broken on real GPU hardware -- asked Joe to check on his actual machine before chasing this further.

v1.2.0 - 2026-08-10 - Fixed the actual bug Joe was reacting to: buildings in the negative-space view looked like solid objects instead of voids. The underlying geometry was already the correct inverse (bounding volume minus buildings, verified by matching triangle counts against a Node harness) -- the problem was purely visual, not geometric. A single DoubleSide red material rendered a carved-out tower cavity's interior walls identically to the solid mass's true outer skin, so a hole and a solid looked the same. Fixed by splitting into two materials sharing the same geometry: outerSkin (FrontSide, terracotta -- the true solid-mass boundary) and cavityWalls (BackSide, near-black -- only visible when looking into a hollow, i.e. where a real building stands). negativeMesh is now a THREE.Group of the two meshes instead of a single Mesh; existing .visible toggling on it (buildings/negative-space view switch) needed no changes since Group supports the same API. Verified live via headless Chrome at three section heights: buildings now read unambiguously as dark voids capped at their real height, with solid red mass everywhere else -- confirmed against Joe's own screenshot showing the ambiguous both-look-solid version this replaces.

v1.1.1 - 2026-08-10 - Fixed CSG artifacts (a stray white sliver shard, faint stray lines across the section-cut floor) that Joe spotted live in the rendered mesh. Root cause: computing the negative space as 497 sequential SUBTRACTION operations (bounding volume minus building 1, minus building 2, ... minus building 497) compounds floating-point drift on every step, since each operation rebuilds its BVH on top of the previous result's own accumulated imprecision. Fixed per Joe's diagnosis: union all 497 building solids into one clean solid first (ADDITION), then do exactly ONE final SUBTRACTION of that merged solid against the bounding volume. Verified in a Node harness on the same input: degenerate/near-zero-area triangles dropped from 50.3% of the mesh (14,897 of 29,591) to 31.4% (8,793 of 28,002) -- real, measurable improvement, not just visually plausible. Confirmed live via headless Chrome: stray white shard from v1.1.0's screenshot no longer present. Progress indicator now reports "Merging buildings N/497" then "Subtracting merged solid from site volume" instead of "Subtracting N/497", to reflect the new two-phase approach.

v1.1.0 - 2026-08-10 - Added a live horizontal section-cut slider (0m to bounding-cap height) to make the negative-space mesh actually legible -- v1.0.x's negative-space view only showed the exterior top of the bounding volume (a featureless slab), since most buildings are far shorter than the max-height cap. Uses renderer.localClippingEnabled + a THREE.Plane on the negative-space material's clippingPlanes, updated live from an input[type=range]. Slider auto-inits to a low cut (min(150m, 40% of cap height)) right after compute finishes, so the carved form is visible immediately instead of requiring the user to discover the slider. Verified live via headless Chrome screenshots at three heights (60m/150m/300m) -- confirmed the towers read as monolithic voids rising out of the solid mass, and higher cuts correctly leave only the supertalls still punching through.

v1.0.1 - 2026-08-10 - Fixed a load-time bug reported live: Joe opened index.html directly via file:// (double-clicked from Explorer) and the app showed "-" for stats with no buildings rendered -- Chrome blocks fetch() of local files from a file:// page even for same-folder assets, so the v1.0.0 fetch('hy_buildings.geojson') / fetch('hy_building_parts.geojson') calls silently failed with no visible error. Fixed by embedding both geojson payloads directly as <script type="application/json"> blocks in index.html and reading them via JSON.parse(el.textContent) instead of fetch -- also means the two separate .geojson files are no longer needed alongside index.html. Verified live via a headless-Chrome run opened at file:// (not http://) with zero console errors and correct stats (497 building solids, 254 from Overture parts).

v1.0.0 - 2026-08-10 - Initial pipeline: extrude Overture building/building_part footprints into stacked solids, subtract from a site-fitted bounding volume via three-bvh-csg. Verified against a Node.js harness (497 solids, 29,591-triangle result, matches browser output exactly). KNOWN ISSUE: negative-space view reads as a solid red slab from outside -- the bounding cap (max building height + 20m) is far taller than most buildings, so the object is dominated by uncut "sky" mass with tower-shaped voids only visible from inside or in cross-section. Needs a clip-plane/section view or transparency before this is actually legible. Not yet fixed.
